# REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning

Zhihong Cui    Hengyu Liu    Haoran Tang Affiliation: Department of
Computing, The Hong Kong Polytechnic University, Hong Kong SAR, China   
Shijun Liu Affiliation: Shandong Key Laboratory of Foundational
Software, School of Software, Shandong University, China    Amir
Taherkordi Affiliation: Department of Informatics, University of Oslo,
Norway    Tor Skeie Affiliation: Department of Informatics, University
of Oslo, Norway Affiliation: Simula Research Laboratory, Norway
Affiliation: Department of Computer Science, Aalborg University, Denmark

###### Abstract

Agent harnesses—runtime scaffolds that wrap an LLM’s reasoning loop with
tool dispatch, verification, and persistent memory—have become the
standard substrate for deploying LLMs as autonomous agents, as
demonstrated by Claude Code and Codex in software engineering. In
trajectory planning, however, this paradigm has not taken hold: existing
LLM-based trajectory planning methods either keep the LLM in the
decision loop, exceeding the millisecond-level control budget, or
offload it to offline rules or training-time supervision—leaving the
runtime planner non-evolving. Intermediate hybrids still lack the
verification-and-consolidation loop that makes harnesses reliable. We
ask: _what runtime harness would let an LLM planner meet real-time
constraints while self-evolving into a deployable one?_ We propose
REINS, a harness built on three LLM–planner couplings: LLM outputs are
(i) grounded to a learned variable-level dynamics model, (ii) verified
via calibrated predicates and forward rollout, and (iii) consolidated
into an indexed rule memory that serves future scenes in
$`\mathcal{O}(\log n)`$ without re-invoking the LLM. The harness closes
a self-evolution loop: familiar scenes resolve by memory lookup; novel
scenes invoke the LLM, whose verified outputs enrich the memory. Without
modifying LLM weights, REINS attains 98%+ compliance, nearly 50%
collision reduction, and 1.9–4.6 ms latency on simulated and real-world
benchmarks at the tactical decision layer, with LLM invocation rate
monotonically declining as memory matures. Code:
[https://github.com/ZhihongCui/REINS](https://github.com/ZhihongCui/REINS).

^(†)^(†)footnotetext: ^(∗)Equal contribution.  ^(†)Corresponding author:
heli@cs.aau.dk

## 1 Introduction

![Refer to caption](2611.10748v1/figures/intro_gaps-v3.png)

Figure 1: Three couplings an agent harness must enforce on the LLM
planner, illustrated on a car-following scene with a 25-m gap. The LLM
utterance “keep a safe distance” is (1) grounded to physical state
variables (TTC, THW, DHW), (2) verified by forward rollout over a
multi-step horizon, and (3) consolidated as an indexed rule keyed by
these variables for instant reuse.

Trajectory planning is the safety-critical core of autonomous driving:
each decision must respect traffic rules, anticipate other agents, and
complete within a millisecond-level control budget (e.g., 50 ms at
20 Hz) ([Zhang et al., 2021a](#bib.bib9); [Falcone et al.,
2007](#bib.bib12); [Lenz et al., 2016](#bib.bib13); [Li et al.,
2024b](#bib.bib23); [Montgomery, 1983](#bib.bib10)). Recent work shows
that large language models (LLMs) bring rich semantic priors over
traffic norms, agent intent, and safety reasoning that classical
planners lack ([Manas et al., 2025](#bib.bib8); [Fu et al.,
2024](#bib.bib15); [Mao et al., 2024](#bib.bib17); [Sha et al.,
2023](#bib.bib16); [Cai et al., 2024](#bib.bib2); [Li et al.,
2024a](#bib.bib1)), yet integrating their reasoning into the runtime
loop is bottlenecked: each LLM query takes seconds—orders of magnitude
over the control budget—and naive integration leaves the planner either
too slow to act or unable to adapt as deployment proceeds.

This is precisely the kind of deployment problem—running a stochastic
LLM inside a low-latency, safety-sensitive loop—that the agent harness
paradigm has recently addressed in software engineering. An agent
harness wraps the LLM with a thin runtime layer of tool dispatch,
intermediate-result verification, persistent memory, and safety
enforcement—as exemplified by systems like Claude Code, Codex, and
Cursor-Agent. On Terminal-Bench ([Merrill et al., 2026](#bib.bib32)),
harness design has emerged as a primary driver of ranking, with
automatic harness optimization surpassing hand-engineered
baselines ([Lee et al., 2026](#bib.bib33)). Trajectory planning,
however, is a sharp test of the paradigm: its millisecond-level control
budget is much tighter than the seconds-to-minutes scale at which coding
harnesses operate, and the safety stakes are higher. In this regime, the
harness’s generic functions specialize into three couplings between LLM
and planner, illustrated in
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"):
when an LLM suggests “keep a safe distance” in a car-following scene,
the runtime must (i) _ground_ the suggestion to physical state variables
(TTC, THW, DHW), (ii) _verify_ the resulting maneuver via forward
rollout over a multi-step horizon, and (iii) _consolidate_ the verified
maneuver into an indexed rule memory.

Existing LLM-based trajectory planning methods span two extremes plus
intermediate hybrids, none of which constitutes a harness in the above
sense: _runtime invocation_ methods keep the LLM in the decision loop
but exceed the millisecond-level budget by orders of magnitude ([Fu et
al., 2024](#bib.bib15); [Mao et al., 2024](#bib.bib17); [Sha et al.,
2023](#bib.bib16)); _offline extraction_ methods discard the LLM at
runtime and freeze the planner against scenarios the offline corpus did
not anticipate ([Li et al., 2024a](#bib.bib1); [Tang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib4)); and _hybrid_ designs partially orchestrate the LLM at
runtime but stop short of a full harness, lacking explicit verification
of LLM commitments and consolidation of verified ones into reusable
structure ([Cui et al., 2026](#bib.bib34); [Zhang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib35)). Across all three, the LLM’s reasoning is either too
slow to act on or too detached from the loop to evolve with deployment.

This raises our central question: _what runtime harness would let an LLM
planner satisfy real-time constraints while self-evolving into a
deployable one?_ Each of the three couplings is non-trivial under
real-time control: grounding requires bridging natural-language
commitments to the planner’s threshold semantics ([Wang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib18); [Zhai et al., 2025](#bib.bib20)); verification must
catch stochastic, occasionally unsafe LLM outputs before they reach
control ([Manas et al., 2025](#bib.bib8); [Zeng et al.,
2025](#bib.bib19)); and consolidation must escape per-call LLM
latency ([Fu et al., 2024](#bib.bib15); [Mao et al., 2024](#bib.bib17))
via a memory pattern emerging in agentic AI ([Wang et al.,
2023](#bib.bib36); [Zhao et al., 2024](#bib.bib38); [Ma et al.,
2024](#bib.bib37)) but not yet transferred to real-time planning. The
three are also jointly necessary: without grounding, verification has
nothing to check; without verification, consolidation stores unverified
content; without consolidation, every scene re-pays the LLM latency
cost. When all three are addressed, a _self-evolution_ loop emerges:
familiar scenes resolve from the consolidated memory in
$`\mathcal{O}(\log n)`$ time, novel scenes invoke the LLM, whose
verified outputs enrich the memory, and reliance on the LLM decreases
monotonically as memory matures, with no LLM weight update.

We propose REINS, a concrete realization of this harness pattern,
instantiated for trajectory planning in driving, that addresses each
coupling as follows. Grounding. We learn a _world dynamics model_ over a
factored variable space, fusing LLM-derived semantic priors with
data-driven dependency discovery. Group-weighted ridge regression
selects each variable’s parents, and a per-variable Transformer predicts
its evolution under the discovered graph. Every LLM output can then be
projected onto this variable space and rolled out under the learned
dynamics. Verification. We compile each LLM-proposed maneuver into
_calibrated predicates_ with thresholds derived from data (z-score,
quantile, or absolute). Predicates are evaluated against the rollout
produced by the dynamics model, and only commitments whose predicted
trajectory satisfies all guards are released to control. Hallucinated or
unsafe commitments are filtered before any maneuver is issued.
Consolidation. We persist verified commitments in an _indexed rule
memory_ backed by a graph database, keyed by the grounding variables
that triggered them. At runtime, parameterized graph queries retrieve
scene-relevant rules in $`\mathcal{O}(\log n)`$ time without any LLM
call. Recurring scenes are thus served by memory lookup, and the LLM is
invoked only on a miss. Together, the three close the self-evolution
loop: at each scene the planner first consults the memory; on a miss the
LLM is invoked, its output grounded against the dynamics model, verified
by rollout, and—if it passes—committed to memory for the next encounter.
We provide an empirical demonstration that, under such a harness, an LLM
planner monotonically reduces its own LLM-call rate while preserving
safety and compliance. Our contributions can be summarized as:

- •
  We extend the agent-harness paradigm to real-time trajectory planning,
  identifying _three couplings_—grounding, verification,
  consolidation—any such harness must enforce, and instantiate it for
  driving; only the variable schema and the action space are
  domain-specific
  (Appendix [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Scope of the Harness Claim ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
- •
  We design REINS, a harness that instantiates the three couplings via a
  learned world dynamics model, calibrated predicates with rollout
  verification, and an indexed rule memory.
- •
  We provide the first empirical evidence of self-evolution in real-time
  LLM trajectory planning: LLM invocation rate decreases monotonically
  over deployment with no LLM weight update.
- •
  We evaluate REINS on simulated and real-world driving benchmarks at
  the tactical decision layer, achieving 98%+ compliance, nearly 50%
  collision reduction, and 1.9–4.6 ms planning latency—well within the
  real-time control budget.

## 2 Related Work

LLM-Based Trajectory Planning. Recent LLM-based trajectory planners fall
along three lines, each realizing some couplings while leaving others
open. _Runtime invocation_ methods keep the LLM in the decision loop.
DiLu ([Fu et al., 2024](#bib.bib15)) pairs LLM reasoning with an
experience-replay memory; Agent-Driver ([Mao et al., 2024](#bib.bib17))
treats the LLM as an orchestrator over perception and planning tools;
LanguageMPC ([Sha et al., 2023](#bib.bib16)) feeds chain-of-thought
outputs into MPC. These systems gain semantic flexibility, but each LLM
query takes seconds, and any output that reaches the planner is
implicitly trusted, lacking the calibrated checks proposed for
stochastic LLM outputs in safety-critical settings ([Manas et al.,
2025](#bib.bib8); [Zeng et al., 2025](#bib.bib19)).

_Offline extraction_ methods sit at the opposite end. LLaDA ([Li et al.,
2024a](#bib.bib1)) extracts location-specific rules from driver
handbooks, DriReg ([Cai et al., 2024](#bib.bib2)) retrieves regulatory
text via RAG, and Plan-R1 ([Tang et al., 2025](#bib.bib4)) distills
LLM-shaped rewards into a reinforcement-learning policy. Removing the
LLM at runtime buys real-time inference, but the planner becomes static,
unable to consolidate new commitments against learned driving
dynamics ([Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib18); [Zhai et al.,
2025](#bib.bib20)) or to adapt to scenarios the offline corpus did not
anticipate.

_Hybrid_ designs partially orchestrate the LLM at runtime. C-TRAIL ([Cui
et al., 2026](#bib.bib34)) runs a closed-loop Recall–Plan–Update cycle
with trust-weighted MCTS injection; LeAD ([Zhang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib35)) pairs an end-to-end planner with a redundant LLM
reasoning track. These improve over either extreme but lack explicit
verification of LLM commitments and consolidation of verified ones into
reusable structure; none grounds, verifies, and consolidates LLM outputs
jointly under a real-time harness.

Agent Harnesses for LLM Deployment. Outside trajectory planning, the
deployment of LLMs into real-world tasks has converged on the _agent
harness_ pattern: a thin runtime layer that wraps the LLM with tool
dispatch, intermediate-result verification, persistent memory, and
safety enforcement. In autonomous coding, systems such as Claude Code
and Codex turn general-purpose LLMs into reliable software engineers,
and harness design has become a primary driver of ranking on
Terminal-Bench ([Merrill et al., 2026](#bib.bib32); [Lee et al.,
2026](#bib.bib33)). In open-ended decision-making, Voyager ([Wang et
al., 2023](#bib.bib36)) accumulates a skill library of executable code
through curiosity-driven exploration; Eureka ([Ma et al.,
2024](#bib.bib37)) iteratively refines reward functions by evolutionary
search over LLM proposals; ExpeL ([Zhao et al., 2024](#bib.bib38))
distills task-specific experience into a retrievable knowledge base.
These harnesses share three structural elements with REINS—grounding
into a domain primitive space, verification of LLM outputs, and
persistent reuse of verified ones—but none operate under millisecond
real-time budgets nor expose their LLMs to the safety-critical
constraints.

## 3 Preliminaries

We formalize the harness’s primitives: a factored variable space
(Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Driving Environment ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"));
a world dynamics model for _grounding_
(Section [3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 World Dynamics Model ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"));
an executable rule representation for _verification_ and _consolidation_
(Section [3.3](#S3.SS3 "3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"));
and the problem definition
(Section [3.4](#S3.SS4 "3.4 Problem Definition ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Full variable lists, template specifications, and the macro-group
dependency enumeration appear in
Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### 3.1 Driving Environment

###### Definition 3.1 (Factored Variable Structure).

A _factored variable structure_ is
$`(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}})`$:
$`\mathcal{X}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{d}\}`$ are observable variables (e.g.,
TTC, THW, DHW, ego*speed, lead_speed, road_friction);
$`\mathcal{H}=\{\textsc{Env},\textsc{Traffic},\textsc{Ego},\textsc{Action},\textsc{Outcome}\}`$
groups them by semantic role ([Chen et al., 2025](#bib.bib7));
$`\mathcal{E}*{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{H}`$
assigns each variable to a group; and
$`\mathcal{E}\_{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{H}`$
specifies permissible group-level dependencies
(Eq. [10](#A1.E10 "Equation 10 ‣ A.1 Variable Definitions ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
in
Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1 "A.1 Variable Definitions ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

###### Definition 3.2 (State and Action Spaces).

The _state_ $`s_{t}\in\mathcal{S}=\prod_{i}\mathrm{dom}(X_{i})`$ is a
complete variable assignment. The _action space_
$`\mathcal{A}=\{\texttt{LANE\_LEFT},\texttt{LANE\_RIGHT},\texttt{FASTER},\texttt{SLOWER},\texttt{IDLE}\}`$
is a finite set of discrete meta-actions for the ego vehicle.

The meta-actions are the interface of the tactical (behavior-planning)
layer in the standard stack decomposition ([Paden et al.,
2016](#bib.bib45)); continuous low-level controllers execute them, and
all safety reasoning below (TTC, headway, accelerations) remains
continuous.

### 3.2 World Dynamics Model

###### Definition 3.3 (Dependency Graph and Dynamics).

A _dependency graph_ is a weighted DAG
$`\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{E},w)`$ with
$`w:\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{R}`$, where edges
$`(X_{i},X_{j})\in\mathcal{E}`$ are admissible only if
$`(h(X_{i}),h(X_{j}))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}`$ ([Giamattei et al.,
2024](#bib.bib6)). A _dynamics function set_
$`\mathcal{F}=\{f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}`$ predicts each variable from its
parents over a temporal lag $`L`$ ([Peters et al., 2017](#bib.bib5)):

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\hat{X}_{i}^{t+1}=f_{i}\big(\mathrm{Pa}(X_{i})^{t-L+1:t}\big),\quad\mathrm{Pa}(X_{i})=\{X_{j}\mid(X_{j},X_{i})\in\mathcal{E}\}.
``` |  | (1) |

An $`H`$-step rollout from $`s_{t}`$ under $`(a_{t},\ldots,a_{t+H-1})`$
yields $`\hat{\xi}=(\hat{s}_{t+1},\ldots,\hat{s}_{t+H})`$.

### 3.3 Rule Representation

###### Definition 3.4 (Predicate).

A *predicate* is a Boolean function
$`p(s)=\mathbb{I}[s[X_{i}]\prec\tau]`$ with comparison operator
$`\prec\in\{<,>,\leq,\geq\}`$ and threshold $`\tau\in\mathbb{R}`$.

TTC and THW guards are the surrogate safety measures of traffic
engineering ([Gettman and Head, 2003](#bib.bib43)); their calibrated
thresholds act as RSS-style safe-distance bounds ([Shalev-Shwartz et
al., 2017](#bib.bib44)).

###### Definition 3.5 (Rule Template).

A *rule template* $`T`$ specifies how predicates compose ([Manas et al.,
2024](#bib.bib11)). We use seven templates: IF-THEN, AND-THEN,
THRESHOLD, PRIORITY, MULTI-ACTION, WEIGHTED-ACTION, and TEMPORAL-CHAIN
(specifications and examples in
Appendix [A.2](#A1.SS2 "A.2 Rule Templates ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

###### Definition 3.6 (Executable Rule).

An *executable rule* is a tuple $`r=(T,\mathcal{P}_{r},a_{r},c_{r})`$
with template $`T`$, guard predicates $`\mathcal{P}_{r}`$, recommended
action $`a_{r}\in\mathcal{A}`$, and confidence $`c_{r}\in[0,1]`$.
Activation requires all guards to hold:

|     |                                                                 |     |     |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \mathrm{Exec}(r,s_{t})=\bigwedge_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{r}}p(s_{t}).
       ```                                                              |     | (2) |

### 3.4 Problem Definition

###### Definition 3.7 (Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Trajectory Planning).

Let $`\mathcal{D}=\{(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1})\}`$ be a driving dataset and
$`\mathcal{M}`$ a pre-trained LLM. The harness problem is to construct
$`\langle\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{F}},\mathcal{R},\pi\rangle`$
where $`\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$ are learned from
$`\mathcal{D}`$ with priors from $`\mathcal{M}`$
(Def. [3.3](#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Definition 3.3 (Dependency Graph and Dynamics). ‣ 3.2 World Dynamics Model ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
$`\mathcal{R}`$ is an indexed rule memory compiled offline from
$`\mathcal{M}`$
(Def. [3.6](#S3.Thmtheorem6 "Definition 3.6 (Executable Rule). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
and $`\pi:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}`$ is a policy that, at each
$`s_{t}`$, retrieves
$`\mathcal{R}_{t}=\mathrm{Retrieve}(s_{t},\mathcal{R})\subseteq\mathcal{R}`$
and proceeds as follows:

- •
  (*hit*) if $`\mathcal{R}_{t}\neq\emptyset`$, $`\pi`$ scores actions
  over $`\mathcal{R}_{t}`$ and verifies them by rollout under
  $`\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$ within a real-time budget;
- •
  (*miss*) otherwise, $`\mathcal{M}`$ proposes a candidate commitment,
  which is grounded via $`(\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{F}})`$ and
  verified by rollout; if all predicates pass, the commitment is
  compiled into a rule $`r`$ and
  $`\mathcal{R}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\cup\{r\}`$.

The parameters of $`\mathcal{M}`$ remain unchanged, and $`\mathcal{R}`$
is monotone non-decreasing; under stationarity of the scene
distribution, the per-scene LLM invocation rate
$`\Pr[\mathcal{R}_{t}=\emptyset]`$ is non-increasing.

## 4 Methodology

![Refer to
caption](2611.10748v1/figures/CLEAR_framework_exampleFinal-v5.png)

Figure 2: The REINS framework. *Phase 1 (offline)*: learn dynamics
$`\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$ and compile rules into initial
memory $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$. *Phase 2 (online)*: per-scene retrieval and
rollout-based verification produce control; on a miss, the LLM proposes
a rule that is grounded, verified, and written back to $`\mathcal{R}`$,
closing the self-evolution loop without any LLM weight update.

REINS realizes the harness as a two-phase system
(Figure [2](#S4.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
*Offline Preparation*
(Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Offline Preparation ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
prepares the world dynamics $`\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$ for
*grounding* and an initial rule memory $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$ for offline
*consolidation*. *Online Runtime*
(Section [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
executes a per-scene loop: rule-guided planning performs retrieval,
scoring, and *verification* over $`\mathcal{R}`$, and online rule
acquisition handles memory misses by querying $`\mathcal{M}`$ and
writing verified rules back to $`\mathcal{R}`$, closing the
*self-evolution loop*.

### 4.1 Offline Preparation

#### 4.1.1 World Dynamics Learning

This module realizes *grounding*: we jointly learn dependency graph
$`\hat{\mathcal{G}}`$ and dynamics functions $`\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$ by
combining LLM semantic priors with data-driven structure discovery. The
full derivation and the dynamics loss are in
Appendix [A.4.1](#A1.SS4.SSS1 "A.4.1 World Dynamics Learning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Dependency Graph. The LLM provides edge confidence $`\pi_{ij}\in[0,1]`$
for each candidate edge $`X_{j}\to X_{i}`$
(Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"));
combined with macro-group constraints
(Eq. [10](#A1.E10 "Equation 10 ‣ A.1 Variable Definitions ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
the unified prior is
$`\tilde{\pi}_{ij}=\alpha\,\pi_{ij}+(1-\alpha)\,\mathbb{I}\{(h(X_{j}),h(X_{i}))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\}`$.
For each $`X_{i}`$, prior-guided ridge regression ([Hoerl and Kennard,
1970](#bib.bib31)) predicts its value from lagged neighbors with
adaptive penalty
$`\lambda_{ij}=\lambda_{0}(1-\tilde{\pi}_{ij})^{\rho}`$, so high-prior
edges are weakly regularized:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{w}}\sum_{t=L}^{N}\Big(X_{i}^{t}-\textstyle\sum_{j\neq i,\ell}w_{j\ell}X_{j}^{t-\ell}\Big)^{2}+\sum_{j\neq i}\lambda_{ij}\|\mathbf{w}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}.
``` |  | (3) |

$`\mathrm{Pa}(X_{i})`$ retains the top-$`K`$ variables by
$`\sum_{\ell}|\hat{w}_{j\ell}|`$, yielding $`\hat{\mathcal{E}}`$ and
weights $`\hat{w}(X_{j},X_{i})=\sum_{\ell}|\hat{w}_{j\ell}|`$.

Dynamics Functions. Given $`\hat{\mathcal{G}}`$, each $`f_{i}`$ is a
multi-head Transformer ([Vaswani et al., 2017](#bib.bib28)) trained to
predict $`X_{i}^{t+1}`$ from $`\mathrm{Pa}(X_{i})^{t-L+1:t}`$ via mean
squared error (loss form in
Eq. [14](#A1.E14 "Equation 14 ‣ A.4.1 World Dynamics Learning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Training curves and hyperparameters are in
Appendix [C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Training Convergence ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

#### 4.1.2 Rule Compilation

This module realizes offline *consolidation*: compiling LLM knowledge
into executable rules. Full equations and per-step prose are in
Appendix [A.4.2](#A1.SS4.SSS2 "A.4.2 Rule Compilation Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Rule Structure Generation. For each edge
$`(X_{j},X_{i})\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}`$, the LLM generates a rule
$`r=(T,\mathcal{P}_{r},a_{r},c_{r})`$
(Eq. [15](#A1.E15 "Equation 15 ‣ A.4.2 Rule Compilation Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
with template $`T`$
(Def. [3.5](#S3.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 3.5 (Rule Template). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
guard predicates $`\mathcal{P}_{r}`$ with symbolic thresholds,
recommended action $`a_{r}\in\mathcal{A}`$, and confidence $`c_{r}`$
derived from edge weight $`\hat{w}(X_{j},X_{i})`$ (prompts in
Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

Threshold Calibration. Each predicate’s threshold $`\tau_{i}`$ is
calibrated from $`\mathcal{D}`$ in three forms ([Luo et al.,
2024](#bib.bib25))—z-score $`\mu_{i}+\zeta\sigma_{i}`$, quantile
$`Q_{i}(\zeta)`$, or absolute constant $`\tau_{0}`$—and the comparison
operator $`\prec`$ is selected to maximize separability of safety
outcome $`y\in\{0,1\}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\prec^{*}=\arg\max_{\prec\in\{<,>\}}\big|\mathbb{E}[y\mid X_{i}\prec\tau_{i}]-\mathbb{E}[y\mid X_{i}\not\prec\tau_{i}]\big|.
``` |  | (4) |

Rule activation then follows
Eq. [2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ Definition 3.6 (Executable Rule). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Graph Database Storage. Compiled rules are stored in a Neo4j graph
database ([Webber, 2012](#bib.bib27)) with rule nodes connected to
variable nodes via TRIGGERS (input) and AFFECTS (output) edges, enabling
$`O(\log|\mathcal{R}|)`$ retrieval. The full schema
(Figure [3](#A1.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ A.4.2 Rule Compilation Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
is in
Appendix [A.4.2](#A1.SS4.SSS2 "A.4.2 Rule Compilation Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning");
database statistics are in
Appendix [C.7.1](#A3.SS7.SSS1 "C.7.1 Rule Database Statistics ‣ C.7 Compiled Rule Database ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### 4.2 Online Runtime

#### 4.2.1 Rule-Guided Planning

This module realizes the runtime planning loop of
Def. [3.7](#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 3.7 (Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Trajectory Planning). ‣ 3.4 Problem Definition ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")—retrieval,
scoring, and *verification* (details in
Appendix [A.4.3](#A1.SS4.SSS3 "A.4.3 Rule-Guided Planning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

Scene-Aware Retrieval. Given $`s_{t}`$, active variables $`\{X_{i}\}`$
trigger parameterized Cypher queries ([Francis et al.,
2018](#bib.bib26)) generated by the LLM (read-only, schema-constrained;
Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
The retrieval map
$`G:(\{X_{i}\},K)\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{R}`$
(Eq. [17](#A1.E17 "Equation 17 ‣ A.4.3 Rule-Guided Planning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
returns up to $`K`$ rules ranked by confidence, restricted to triggers
whose dependency direction respects the macro-group constraints.

Action Scoring. For each $`r\in\mathcal{R}_{t}`$ activated via
Eq. [2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ Definition 3.6 (Executable Rule). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"),
a Transformer-based scorer ([Liang et al., 2022](#bib.bib29)) produces
relevance $`S_{\text{rel}}(r,s_{t})\in[0,1]`$ and action preference
$`S_{\text{act}}(r,s_{t},a)`$, yielding combined score
$`f(r,a\mid s_{t})=S_{\text{rel}}\cdot S_{\text{act}}\cdot c_{r}`$
(Eqs. [18](#A1.E18 "Equation 18 ‣ A.4.3 Rule-Guided Planning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")–[19](#A1.E19 "Equation 19 ‣ A.4.3 Rule-Guided Planning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning");
training loss in
Eq. [20](#A1.E20 "Equation 20 ‣ A.4.3 Rule-Guided Planning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Multiple rules recommending action $`a`$, denoted
$`\mathcal{R}_{t}(a)=\{r\in\mathcal{R}_{t}\mid a_{r}=a\}`$, are
aggregated by noisy-OR:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P(a\mid s_{t})\;\propto\;1-\prod_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{t}(a)}\big(1-f(r,a\mid s_{t})\big).
``` |  | (5) |

The full normalized form and argmax decoder are in
Appendix [A.4.3](#A1.SS4.SSS3 "A.4.3 Rule-Guided Planning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Trajectory Verification. The learned dynamics $`\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$
verifies safety via forward rollout. For the top-$`M`$ actions ranked by
$`P(a\mid s_{t})`$, we predict $`H`$-step trajectories
$`\hat{\xi}(a)=(\hat{s}_{t+1},\ldots,\hat{s}_{t+H})`$ via
$`\hat{s}_{t+h+1}=\Phi(\hat{s}_{t+h},a;\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{F}})`$
(Eq. [22](#A1.E22 "Equation 22 ‣ A.4.3 Rule-Guided Planning Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
and select
$`a^{\star}_{t}=\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{M}}J(\hat{\xi}(a))`$, where
$`J`$ penalizes collisions, rule violations, and control effort
(Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Evaluation Metrics ‣ Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"));
the first control is executed in receding-horizon ([Zhang et al.,
2021b](#bib.bib30)).

#### 4.2.2 Online Rule Acquisition

This module realizes
Def. [3.7](#S3.Thmtheorem7 "Definition 3.7 (Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Trajectory Planning). ‣ 3.4 Problem Definition ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")’s
miss-handling path through four steps (full equations and the
monotonicity argument in
Appendix [A.4.4](#A1.SS4.SSS4 "A.4.4 Online Rule Acquisition Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

Miss Detection. We operationalize the miss criterion
($`\mathcal{R}_{t}=\emptyset`$) with an additional confidence guard
against weakly supported matches:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathrm{Miss}(s_{t})\;=\;\mathbb{I}\!\left[\mathcal{R}_{t}=\emptyset\right]\;\vee\;\mathbb{I}\!\left[\max_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{t},\,a\in\mathcal{A}}f(r,a\mid s_{t})<\eta\right],
``` |  | (6) |

with tuned threshold $`\eta\in(0,1)`$.

Rule Proposal and Online Verification. On a miss, $`\mathcal{M}`$
proposes
$`\tilde{r}=(\tilde{T},\tilde{\mathcal{P}},\tilde{a},\tilde{c})`$
(Eq. [23](#A1.E23 "Equation 23 ‣ A.4.4 Online Rule Acquisition Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
conforming to
Defs. [3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Definition 3.4 (Predicate). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")–[3.5](#S3.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 3.5 (Rule Template). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
(Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Symbolic thresholds in $`\tilde{\mathcal{P}}`$ are calibrated on a
sliding window $`\mathcal{D}_{t}^{W}=\{s_{t-W+1},\ldots,s_{t}\}`$ so
they track the current distribution. The harness then rolls out
$`\tilde{a}`$ from $`s_{t}`$ under $`\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$ and accepts
$`\tilde{r}`$ iff every predicate holds along the rollout:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathrm{Accept}(\tilde{r},s_{t})\;=\;\bigwedge_{p\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}}\bigwedge_{h=1}^{H}p(\hat{s}_{t+h}).
``` |  | (7) |

On rejection the planner falls back to $`\arg\max_{a}P(a\mid s_{t})`$
over $`\mathcal{R}_{t}`$ if $`\mathcal{R}_{t}\neq\emptyset`$; otherwise
the harness raises a human-takeover alert and executes the safest action
under $`\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$ by maximizing minimum lookahead TTC
(Eq. [24](#A1.E24 "Equation 24 ‣ A.4.4 Online Rule Acquisition Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
in
Appendix [A.4.4](#A1.SS4.SSS4 "A.4.4 Online Rule Acquisition Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

Memory Update. Accepted $`\tilde{r}`$ is keyed by content hash
$`\kappa(\tilde{r}):=\kappa(\tilde{T},\tilde{\mathcal{P}},\tilde{a})`$
and inserted into Neo4j; on collision, confidences are averaged instead
of duplicating the node:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{R}\;\longleftarrow\;\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}\cup\{\tilde{r}\}&\kappa(\tilde{r})\notin\mathrm{Keys}(\mathcal{R}),\\[2.0pt]
\mathcal{R}\text{ with }c_{r^{\prime}}\leftarrow\tfrac{1}{2}(c_{r^{\prime}}+\tilde{c})&\exists\,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}:\kappa(r^{\prime})=\kappa(\tilde{r}).\end{cases}
``` |  | (8) |

The collision-branch rationale (averaging vs. duplication, exclusion of
$`\tilde{c}`$ from $`\kappa`$) is in
Appendix [A.4.4](#A1.SS4.SSS4 "A.4.4 Online Rule Acquisition Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Self-Evolution Properties.
Eq. [8](#S4.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ 4.2.2 Online Rule Acquisition ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
structurally enforces $`|\mathcal{R}_{t+1}|\geq|\mathcal{R}_{t}|`$
(memory monotonicity) and
$`\theta_{\mathcal{M}}\equiv\theta_{\mathcal{M}}^{(0)}`$ (no LLM
update). Under stationarity of the scene distribution, the per-state
miss rate is non-increasing as $`\mathcal{R}`$ accumulates coverage:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
|\mathcal{R}_{t+1}|\geq|\mathcal{R}_{t}|,\quad\Pr[\mathrm{Miss}(s_{t+1})]\leq\Pr[\mathrm{Miss}(s_{t})],\quad\theta_{\mathcal{M}}\equiv\theta_{\mathcal{M}}^{(0)},
``` |  | (9) |

so the per-scene LLM invocation rate decays monotonically with
$`\Pr[\mathrm{Miss}]`$ (full argument in
Appendix [A.4.4](#A1.SS4.SSS4 "A.4.4 Online Rule Acquisition Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning");
validated in
Section [5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

## 5 Experiment

The main body reports overall comparison
(Section [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Performance Evaluation ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
ablation
(Section [5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Ablation Study ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
self-evolution dynamics on Highway
(Section [5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
and a summary of the robustness, transfer, and scalability results.
Appendices [C](#A3 "Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
and [D](#A4 "Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
report trajectory accuracy, sensitivity, extended self-evolution traces,
a case study, the rule database, and the fairness and robustness
studies.

### 5.1 Experiment Setting

Datasets and Protocols. We evaluate REINS on four highway-env ([Leurent,
2018](#bib.bib24)) scenarios—Highway, Merge, Roundabout, Intersection
(100 steps at 5 Hz, 15 observable vehicles per episode)—and two
drone-captured real-world datasets ([Krajewski et al.,
2018](#bib.bib21); [Krajewski et al., 2020](#bib.bib22)): highD (German
highway, 4,237 vehicles, 578 lane changes) and rounD (German
roundabouts, 2,156 vehicles, 423 yield/merge events). Surrounding
vehicles in simulation are *reactive* (IDM car-following ([Treiber et
al., 2000](#bib.bib47)) with MOBIL lane changes ([Kesting et al.,
2007](#bib.bib48))), so they brake, yield, and change lanes in response
to the ego. The real-world datasets run in *closed-loop replay*: one
recorded vehicle is controlled by the planner while the others follow
their recorded trajectories, so these rows reflect human behavior rather
than a simulator’s; a collision is registered when bounding boxes
overlap or the ego leaves the drivable lane. A reactive-replay variant
following the nuPlan protocol ([Caesar et al., 2021](#bib.bib42)) is in
Appendix [D.6](#A4.SS6 "D.6 Reactive Replay and Observation Noise ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").
All methods observe clean factored variables; perception, tracking, and
map errors lie outside the evaluated layer
(Section [6](#S6 "6 Limitations ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

Evaluation Metrics. Compl. (Rule Compliance) is the fraction of steps at
which all active rules hold, measured for each method against its own
executable rule set; an audit of all methods against REINS’s compiled
set as a common reference leaves the ordering unchanged
(Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
RE (Risk-aware Efficiency) is task reward minus a TTC-based risk
penalty. CR (Collision Rate) is the fraction of *episodes* with at least
one contact among the 15 vehicles over 100 steps, an episode-level rate
in deliberately stressed traffic. PE (Planning Efficiency) is the mean
wall-clock ms of the synchronous action-selection computation per step
for every method; for REINS it excludes the asynchronous
rule-acquisition pipeline, profiled separately in
Appendix [D.8](#A4.SS8 "D.8 Latency of the Online-Miss Cycle and Its Scheduling ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").
TTC and THW, which underlie the guards and the RE penalty, are the
surrogate safety measures of traffic engineering ([Gettman and Head,
2003](#bib.bib43)), and Compl. and CR echo the rule-conformance and
collision outcomes reported in scenario-based safety
assessment ([Riedmaier et al., 2020](#bib.bib46)). Results are averaged
over five seeds; formal definitions in
Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Evaluation Metrics ‣ Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Baselines. We compare with eight baselines covering the regimes from
Section [2](#S2 "2 Related Work ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
plus classical anchors: (i) *classical* (MPC ([Falcone et al.,
2007](#bib.bib12)), MCTS ([Lenz et al., 2016](#bib.bib13))); (ii)
*runtime invocation* (DriveSpeak ([Cui et al., 2024](#bib.bib14)));
(iii) *offline extraction* (LLaDA ([Li et al., 2024a](#bib.bib1)),
DriReg ([Cai et al., 2024](#bib.bib2)), Plan-R1 ([Tang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib4))); (iv) *symbolic/hybrid* (PDDL ([Guan et al.,
2023](#bib.bib39); [Liu et al., 2023](#bib.bib40)), SymAgent ([Liu et
al., 2025](#bib.bib3))). All methods share the observation space, action
space, episodes, and LLM backbone (GPT-4o, temperature 0); descriptions,
the shared and unshared conditions, and a matched re-run of PDDL and
DriReg over REINS’s vocabulary and calibration pipeline are in
Appendices [B.3](#A2.SS3 "B.3 Baseline Descriptions ‣ Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
and [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

| Dataset | Scenario | Metric | DriveSpeak | LLaDA | MPC | MCTS | PDDL | DriReg | SymAgent | Plan-R1 | REINS |

|  |  | CR ($`\downarrow`$,%) | 41.23 | 38.56 | 25.34 | 23.12 | 26.78 | 24.23 | 20.89 | 18.47 | 9.45 |

Table 1: Performance comparison across simulated scenarios and
real-world datasets. Methods without explicit rule representations (MPC,
MCTS, DriveSpeak, LLaDA) cannot report Compliance against their own
rules as they lack executable rule constraints; their compliance against
REINS’s compiled set as a common reference is given in
Table [15](#A4.T15 "Table 15 ‣ D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### 5.2 Performance Evaluation

Performance evaluation results are reported in
Table [1](#S5.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 5.1 Experiment Setting ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"),
with trajectory accuracy analysis in
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Trajectory Accuracy ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Effectiveness Analysis. (i) REINS beats all baselines, improving over
Plan-R1 by 2.4%–3.6% Compl., 3.06–5.48 RE, and 49%–56% CR
reduction—validating that grounding, verification, and consolidation act
jointly. (ii) All methods incur higher CR on complex scenarios
(Roundabout, Intersection) than structured ones (Highway, Merge); REINS
degrades least, owing to context-aware retrieval. (iii) On highD and
rounD, REINS maintains Compl. above 0.98 with 6.6–9.0-point CR reduction
over Plan-R1, transferring to naturalistic driving without
domain-specific tuning. (iv) Executable rule methods (PDDL, DriReg,
SymAgent, Plan-R1) beat language-guided ones (DriveSpeak, LLaDA, with
22%–44% CR for ungrounded rules), yet their static thresholds and
uniform activation still trail REINS’s data-driven calibration and
variable-indexed retrieval; a matched re-run over REINS’s vocabulary and
calibration narrows PDDL’s and DriReg’s CR by 3–5 points but leaves the
gap intact
(Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
(v) Classical planners (MPC, MCTS) are fast but unsafe (CR 14%–28%);
Plan-R1 is the best baseline but plateaus under uniform rule
application.

Efficiency Analysis. REINS completes planning in 1.9–4.6 ms per step, up
to 58% faster than runtime LLM baselines (DriveSpeak, LLaDA) and 15–33%
faster than Plan-R1. Two factors contribute: (i) variable-indexed
retrieval activates only 2–3 rules per step via $`O(\log|\mathcal{R}|)`$
queries, and (ii) learned dynamics enable closed-form rollout without
iterative LLM calls. PDDL makes no runtime LLM call either, yet
re-grounds every precondition at each step (3.71 of its 4.95 ms on
Highway;
Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Runtime Profile of the PDDL Baseline ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
exactly the cost that indexed consolidation removes.

|      | Metric                  | w/o     | w/o LLM | w/o Rule   | w/o       | Full    |
|------|-------------------------|---------|---------|------------|-----------|---------|
|      |                         | World   | Prior   | Generation | Retrieval |         |

|      | RE ($`\uparrow`$)       | -1.9234 | -1.5390 | -2.4156    | -0.8921   | 0.5228  |
|      | CR ($`\downarrow`$,%)   | 8.47    | 8.26    | 11.23      | 8.12      | 5.67    |
|      | PE ($`\downarrow`$, ms) | 2.9532  | 2.6831  | 2.5124     | 3.8921    | 2.4998  |
| Mer. | Compl. ($`\uparrow`$)   | 0.9687  | 0.9738  | 0.9198     | 0.9812    | 0.9924  |
|      | RE ($`\uparrow`$)       | -1.5621 | -1.2619 | -2.1893    | -0.7234   | -0.0400 |
|      | CR ($`\downarrow`$,%)   | 9.23    | 9.08    | 12.87      | 8.89      | 6.86    |
|      | PE ($`\downarrow`$, ms) | 2.6234  | 2.1832  | 1.9021     | 2.9834    | 1.8928  |
| Rou. | Compl. ($`\uparrow`$)   | 0.9534  | 0.9611  | 0.9087     | 0.9756    | 0.9925  |
|      | RE ($`\uparrow`$)       | -2.3456 | -1.1783 | -3.1234    | -1.2134   | -0.5444 |
|      | CR ($`\downarrow`$,%)   | 12.34   | 11.84   | 15.89      | 11.23     | 9.01    |
|      | PE ($`\downarrow`$, ms) | 4.1245  | 3.8011  | 3.6521     | 5.2134    | 3.6243  |
| Int. | Compl. ($`\uparrow`$)   | 0.9456  | 0.9582  | 0.8923     | 0.9689    | 0.9838  |
|      | RE ($`\uparrow`$)       | -2.5678 | -1.9812 | -3.4521    | -1.3456   | -0.5747 |

Table 2: Ablation study across four driving scenarios.

### 5.3 Ablation Study

We conduct ablation experiments on all four simulated scenarios to
evaluate component contributions.
Table [2](#S5.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 5.2 Performance Evaluation ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
reports results for four variants: (i) w/o World Model: replacing
learned dynamics with constant-velocity propagation; (ii) w/o LLM Prior:
using purely data-driven structure learning without semantic guidance;
(iii) w/o Rule Generation: substituting LLM-generated rules with
hand-crafted ones; (iv) w/o Retrieval: applying all rules uniformly
without context-aware selection.

Removing the world dynamics model causes the largest safety degradation
(CR +49%/+48% on Highway/Intersection), confirming multi-step prediction
is essential. Without LLM priors, CR rises 31–46%, showing that semantic
guidance regularizes dependency discovery beyond purely data-driven
methods. Hand-crafted rules yield the largest compliance drop
(0.9956$`\to`$0.9312 on Highway) and nearly double CR, exposing the
limited coverage of manual engineering; even so, they remain safer
inside the harness than every matched no-harness baseline
(Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Disabling context-aware retrieval raises both CR (22–43%) and PE
(34–58%) via conflicting constraints. The four roles are complementary:
dynamics enables predictive verification, LLM priors regularize
structure, rule generation ensures coverage, and selective retrieval
prevents conflicts.

### 5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis

| Checkpoint | $`|\mathcal{R}|`$ | LLM Call Rate (%) | Compl. ($`\uparrow`$) | CR (%) ($`\downarrow`$) |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| ep. 250 | 113 | 70.0 | 0.9885 | 7.05 |
| ep. 500 | 127 | 48.44 | 0.9920 | 6.11 |
| ep. 750 | 138 | 25.0 | 0.9942 | 5.83 |
| ep. 1000 (final) | 145 | 12.81 | 0.9956 | 5.67 |
| Frozen $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$ (1000-ep eval) | 88 | — | 0.9800 | 9.63 |

Table 3: Self-evolution dynamics on Highway over 1000 episodes; LLM call
rate is the miss rate
(Eq. [6](#S4.E6 "Equation 6 ‣ 4.2.2 Online Rule Acquisition ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
in the 250-episode window ending at each checkpoint.

We deploy REINS for 1000 episodes on Highway from the offline-compiled
memory $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$ ($`|\mathcal{R}_{0}|{=}88`$), reporting four
checkpoint snapshots alongside a *Frozen $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$* control
that runs the same window with acquisition disabled
(Table [3](#S5.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning");
other scenarios in
Appendix [C.5](#A3.SS5 "C.5 Self-Evolution Across Scenarios ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Across deployment, $`|\mathcal{R}|`$ grows from 88 to 145 (saturating
after ep. 500;
Eq. [9](#S4.E9 "Equation 9 ‣ 4.2.2 Online Rule Acquisition ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
the LLM call rate decays from 70.0% to 12.81% (a 5.5$`\times`$ reduction
with no weight update), Compl. rises from 0.9885 to 0.9956, and CR drops
from 7.05% to 5.67%. Against the Frozen $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$ control over
the same horizon (0.9800 Compl., 9.63% CR), online acquisition adds
$`+0.0156`$ Compl. and removes 3.96 CR points—the headline
self-evolution gain. Mapped to the three couplings: monotone
$`|\mathcal{R}|`$ under $`\kappa`$-hash deduplication validates
consolidation, the joint Compl. $`\uparrow`$/CR $`\downarrow`$
trajectory validates rollout-based verification, and the 5.5$`\times`$
call-rate drop validates the grounding–consolidation loop, as
variable-keyed memory hits replace LLM calls on similar scenes.

Of the roughly 39,000 proposals implied by these call rates, 2.1% failed
schema validation, 18.0% were rejected by rollout verification, and the
accepted remainder deduplicated to 57 unique rules, 54 of which an audit
judged consistent with domain guidelines
(Appendix [D.5](#A4.SS5 "D.5 What Rollout Verification Filters ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability.
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
stresses these results. Rules regenerated with Claude Sonnet 4.5 or
Qwen2.5-72B-Instruct keep Highway CR at 5.89% and 6.24% (vs. 5.67%), and
prompt paraphrases span 5.58–5.91%. Under 2.5–10% observation noise and
5% frame drops, REINS CR rises to at most 8.14% while Plan-R1 rises to
19.87%. A memory compiled only from highD/rounD starts at an 86.2% call
rate on highway-env and recovers to within 0.5–1.1 CR points of the
native memory within 1000 episodes, whereas a frozen transferred memory
does not. Inflating the memory to 100,000 rules keeps retrieval under
0.8 ms, and the 1.59 s miss cycle, dominated by the LLM call, runs off
the control path behind a 2.6 ms verified fallback.

## 6 Limitations

Abstraction gap and action interface. The evaluation sits at the
tactical decision layer: perception is assumed as clean factored
variables, highD and rounD run in replay (reactive-replay variant in
Appendix [D.6](#A4.SS6 "D.6 Reactive Replay and Observation Noise ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
and occlusion, tracking, and map errors are absent; the five
meta-actions are the standard interface of this layer and of all eight
baselines, and continuous maneuvers would change only the scorer’s
output head. The claims are harness results at this layer, not a
deployable AV stack, and the episode-level collision rates of 5.7–9.5%
in deliberately stressed traffic are not deployment grade; the supported
claim is relative (49–56% below the best learned baseline).
Correlational dynamics. The learned dynamics are prior-constrained but
not causally identified; verification is conservative, so misattribution
degrades toward rejecting actions rather than accepting unsafe ones, yet
its quality depends on dynamics fidelity (constant-velocity propagation
raises CR by 48–49%; 3 of the 57 acquired rules were over-specific in
audit,
Appendix [D.5](#A4.SS5 "D.5 What Rollout Verification Filters ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Limited zero-shot cross-domain reusability. Zero-shot cross-domain
reusability of the compiled memory is limited: a memory compiled solely
from highD/rounD begins at an 86.2% call rate on highway-env, indicating
that many target-domain encounters still require online acquisition.
REINS is accordingly scoped to the self-evolution loop rather than
assuming universal cross-domain memory transfer
(Appendix [D.7](#A4.SS7 "D.7 Cross-Domain Memory Transfer ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Scope. The monotone miss-rate property assumes a stationary scene
distribution, and the implementation is specialized to driving: only the
variable schema and the action space must be re-authored for another
domain
(Appendix [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Scope of the Harness Claim ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

## 7 Conclusion

We present REINS, a self-evolving agent harness that adapts the
agent-harness paradigm from software engineering to real-time,
safety-critical trajectory planning through three LLM–planner couplings:
grounding, verification, and consolidation. Familiar scenes resolve from
an indexed rule memory in $`\mathcal{O}(\log|\mathcal{R}|)`$ time, while
memory misses trigger LLM-driven rule acquisition that grounds,
verifies, and writes back new rules, closing the self-evolution loop
without any LLM weight update. At the tactical decision layer, REINS
attains 98%+ compliance, nearly 50% collision reduction, and 1.9–4.6 ms
latency, with gains that survive matched baselines, observation noise,
reactive replay, and cross-domain memory transfer. Future work will
couple the harness to perception uncertainty and continuous maneuvers,
extend it to multi-agent coordination, and move the learned dynamics
toward interventional identification with incremental updates.

## Appendix A More Details about REINS

### A.1 Variable Definitions

We define macro and micro variables shown in
Table [4](#A1.T4 "Table 4 ‣ A.1 Variable Definitions ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").
The macro variables represent high-level semantic categories, while
micro variables are the specific measurable quantities used in the world
dynamics model and rule system.

The variables are organized into five functional groups: Environment
variables capture external conditions (weather, visibility, road
surface, speed limits) that affect driving but are not directly
controllable. Traffic variables describe spatial and temporal
relationships with surrounding vehicles, including headway distances,
relative speeds, TTC, and lane density metrics. Ego variables represent
the controlled vehicle’s kinematic state (position, velocity,
acceleration, orientation). Action defines the discrete action space:
five high-level maneuvers executed by low-level controllers. Outcome
variables measure action consequences, including safety (collision
risk), comfort (jerk-based), and task completion indicators. This
hierarchical organization enables structured rule retrieval via
variable-group indexing.

The macro-group dependency set $`\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}`$ in
Def. [3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 3.1 (Factored Variable Structure). ‣ 3.1 Driving Environment ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
encodes the admissible group-level edges:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}=\{`$ | $`\displaystyle(\textsc{Env},\textsc{Traffic}),(\textsc{Env},\textsc{Ego}),(\textsc{Env},\textsc{Outcome}),`$ |  | (10) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle(\textsc{Traffic},\textsc{Traffic}),(\textsc{Traffic},\textsc{Ego}),(\textsc{Traffic},\textsc{Outcome}),`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle(\textsc{Ego},\textsc{Traffic}),(\textsc{Ego},\textsc{Ego}),(\textsc{Ego},\textsc{Outcome}),`$ |  |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle(\textsc{Action},\textsc{Ego}),(\textsc{Action},\textsc{Outcome})\}.`$ |  |  |

Environment factors influence Traffic, Ego, and Outcome; Traffic and Ego
variables interact bidirectionally and feed into Outcome; Action affects
only Ego state and Outcome.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Macro Variable | Micro Variable | Description |
| Environment | weather_type | Weather condition (clear/rain/fog/snow) |
|  | visibility_distance | Visibility range (m) |
|  | road_friction | Road surface friction coefficient |
|  | road_type | Scenario type (highway/merge/intersection/roundabout) |
|  | time_of_day | Time period (day/night/dawn/dusk) |
|  | speed_limit | Posted speed limit (km/h) |
| Traffic | headway_distance | Distance to lead vehicle (m) |
|  | lead_speed | Lead vehicle speed (m/s) |
|  | lead_rel_speed | Relative speed to lead vehicle (m/s) |
|  | lead_ttc | Time-to-collision with lead vehicle (s) |
|  | lead_thw | Time headway to lead vehicle (s) |
|  | rear_distance | Distance to rear vehicle (m) |
|  | rear_speed | Rear vehicle speed (m/s) |
|  | rear_rel_speed | Relative speed to rear vehicle (m/s) |
|  | rear_ttc | Time-to-collision with rear vehicle (s) |
|  | left_front_distance | Distance to left-front vehicle (m) |
|  | left_front_speed | Left-front vehicle speed (m/s) |
|  | left_rear_distance | Distance to left-rear vehicle (m) |
|  | left_rear_speed | Left-rear vehicle speed (m/s) |
|  | right_front_distance | Distance to right-front vehicle (m) |
|  | right_front_speed | Right-front vehicle speed (m/s) |
|  | right_rear_distance | Distance to right-rear vehicle (m) |
|  | right_rear_speed | Right-rear vehicle speed (m/s) |
|  | lane_density_left | Left lane density (vehicles/100m) |
|  | lane_density_right | Right lane density (vehicles/100m) |
|  | available_gap_left | Available gap in left lane (m) |
|  | available_gap_right | Available gap in right lane (m) |
|  | cutin_risk | Cut-in risk indicator (0–1) |
| Ego | ego_x | Ego vehicle longitudinal position (m) |
|  | ego_y | Ego vehicle lateral position (m) |
|  | ego_vx | Ego vehicle longitudinal velocity (m/s) |
|  | ego_vy | Ego vehicle lateral velocity (m/s) |
|  | ego_speed | Ego vehicle total speed (m/s) |
|  | ego_acceleration | Ego vehicle acceleration (m/s²) |
|  | yaw_rate | Ego vehicle yaw rate (rad/s) |
|  | jerk | Ego vehicle jerk (m/s³) |
|  | lane_id | Current lane identifier |
|  | lateral_offset | Lateral offset from lane center (m) |
| Action | action_type | Discrete action (IDLE/FASTER/SLOWER/LANE_LEFT/LANE_RIGHT) |
| Outcome | risk_score | Collision probability (0–1) |
|  | comfort_score | Comfort metric (jerk-based) |
|  | energy_consumption | Energy usage metric |
|  | success_flag | Goal completion indicator (boolean) |
|  | violation_flag | Rule violation indicator (boolean) |

Table 4: Macro and micro variable definitions used in REINS.

### A.2 Rule Templates

Seven rule templates are defined to capture different logical structures
for driving knowledge:

- •
  IF-THEN: Conditional implication for basic conditional relations.
  Example: IF TTC $`<`$ 2s THEN decelerate
- •
  AND-THEN: Conjunctive trigger requiring multiple conditions.
  Example: IF visibility $`<`$ 50m AND raining THEN reduce_speed_by_30%
- •
  THRESHOLD: Numeric constraint with calibrated boundaries.
  Example: IF speed $`>`$ speed_limit THEN violation = true
- •
  PRIORITY: Decision hierarchy for conflicting actions.
  Example: yield $`\succ`$ accelerate (yield takes priority)
- •
  MULTI-ACTION: Compound actions under one condition.
  Example: IF collision_risk = high THEN brake AND steer_away
- •
  WEIGHTED-ACTION: Weighted action selection.
  Example: IF crash_risk THEN {brake: 0.7, steer: 0.3}
- •
  TEMPORAL-CHAIN: Temporal constraints between triggers.
  Example: IF obstacle_detected THEN brake within 1.5s

### A.3 Rule Conflict Resolution

When multiple rules are activated simultaneously, conflicts may arise if
they recommend different actions. REINS resolves conflicts through a
three-level arbitration mechanism:

Priority-based ordering. Rules are assigned priority levels based on
their functional category: Safety $`>`$ Compliance $`>`$ Navigation
$`>`$ Efficiency. When rules from different categories conflict, the
higher-priority rule takes precedence. For example, if an Efficiency
rule (consider lane change) and a Safety rule (maintain gap) both fire,
the Safety rule overrides.

Confidence-weighted aggregation. For rules within the same priority
level, REINS aggregates their scores using the noisy-OR formula
(Eq. ([5](#S4.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ 4.2.1 Rule-Guided Planning ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))).
This allows multiple supporting rules to reinforce an action while
remaining robust to individual noisy predictions. The action with the
highest aggregated probability is selected.

Dynamics-based verification. When the aggregated action prior is below a
confidence threshold ($`\tau=0.7`$), REINS falls back to dynamics-based
verification. The top-$`M`$ candidate actions are simulated via forward
rollout using the world dynamics model, and the action yielding the
safest predicted trajectory is selected.

This hierarchical approach ensures that safety-critical rules are never
overridden by efficiency considerations, while still allowing flexible
action selection in non-critical situations.

### A.4 Method Details

This subsection contains the full equations, architectural details, and
explanatory prose deferred from the methodology to keep the main body
concise. The structure mirrors
Section [4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Offline Preparation ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")–[4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

#### A.4.1 World Dynamics Learning Details

We learn the dependency graph
$`\hat{\mathcal{G}}=(\mathcal{X},\hat{\mathcal{E}},\hat{w})`$ in three
steps, then train a per-variable dynamics function on the resulting
parent sets.

Step 1: Edge Prior Estimation. We query the LLM with variable dependency
prompts
(Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
to obtain an edge confidence $`\pi_{ij}\in[0,1]`$ for each candidate
edge $`X_{j}\to X_{i}`$. This is combined with the macro-group
constraints
(Eq. [10](#A1.E10 "Equation 10 ‣ A.1 Variable Definitions ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
to form a unified prior:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\tilde{\pi}_{ij}=\alpha\,\pi_{ij}+(1-\alpha)\,\mathbb{I}\{(h(X_{j}),h(X_{i}))\in\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}\},
``` |  | (11) |

where $`\alpha\in[0,1]`$ balances LLM confidence against structural
constraints. Edges that violate the macro-group constraints carry
indicator $`0`$ and are reduced to $`\alpha\,\pi_{ij}`$, while
semantically aligned edges are pushed toward $`1`$.

Step 2: Prior-Guided Regression. For each target variable $`X_{i}`$, we
predict its value from the lagged values of other variables via ridge
regression \[[Hoerl and Kennard, 1970](#bib.bib31)\] with prior-adaptive
penalties $`\lambda_{ij}=\lambda_{0}(1-\tilde{\pi}_{ij})^{\rho}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{\mathbf{w}}_{i}=\arg\min_{\mathbf{w}}\sum_{t=L}^{N}\Big(X_{i}^{t}-\textstyle\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}w_{j\ell}X_{j}^{t-\ell}\Big)^{2}+\sum_{j\neq i}\lambda_{ij}\|\mathbf{w}_{j}\|_{2}^{2},
``` |  | (12) |

where $`w_{j\ell}`$ is the regression coefficient for variable $`X_{j}`$
at lag $`\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}`$,
$`\mathbf{w}_{j}=(w_{j1},\ldots,w_{jL})^{\top}`$ groups all lags for
$`X_{j}`$, $`\lambda_{0}`$ is the base penalty, and $`\rho>0`$ controls
penalty sharpness. High-prior edges ($`\tilde{\pi}_{ij}\to 1`$) receive
weak regularization and survive; low-prior edges are shrunk toward zero.

Step 3: Graph Construction. The parent set $`\mathrm{Pa}(X_{i})`$
retains the top-$`K`$ variables ranked by aggregated coefficient
magnitude $`\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}|\hat{w}_{j\ell}|`$. The learned edge set
and weights are

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{\mathcal{E}}=\{(X_{j},X_{i})\mid X_{j}\in\mathrm{Pa}(X_{i})\},\quad\hat{w}(X_{j},X_{i})=\textstyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}|\hat{w}_{j\ell}|.
``` |  | (13) |

Dynamics Function Loss. Given $`\hat{\mathcal{G}}`$, each $`f_{i}`$ is
implemented as a multi-head Transformer \[[Vaswani et al.,
2017](#bib.bib28)\] and trained by mean squared error over its parent
trajectory:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\text{dyn}}^{(i)}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t}\big(X_{i}^{t+1}-f_{i}(\mathrm{Pa}(X_{i})^{t-L+1:t})\big)^{2}.
``` |  | (14) |

Optimizer, batch size, and convergence curves are reported in
Appendix [C.1](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Training Convergence ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

#### A.4.2 Rule Compilation Details

Rule Structure Generation. For each dependency edge
$`(X_{j},X_{i})\in\hat{\mathcal{E}}`$, the LLM generates a rule
structure

|     |                                    |     |      |
|-----|------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       r=(T,\mathcal{P}_{r},a_{r},c_{r}),
       ```                                 |     | (15) |

where $`T`$ is the rule template
(Def. [3.5](#S3.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 3.5 (Rule Template). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
$`\mathcal{P}_{r}`$ is a set of guard predicates with symbolic
thresholds, $`a_{r}\in\mathcal{A}`$ is the recommended action
(Def. [3.2](#S3.Thmtheorem2 "Definition 3.2 (State and Action Spaces). ‣ 3.1 Driving Environment ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
and $`c_{r}`$ is the confidence derived from edge weight
$`\hat{w}(X_{j},X_{i})`$. Prompt details are in
Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Threshold Calibration. Each predicate $`p\in\mathcal{P}_{r}`$
(Def. [3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Definition 3.4 (Predicate). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
requires a concrete threshold $`\tau_{i}`$, calibrated from
$`\mathcal{D}`$ in three forms \[[Luo et al., 2024](#bib.bib25)\]:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\tau_{i}=\begin{cases}\mu_{i}+\zeta\sigma_{i}&\text{(z-score)},\\
Q_{i}(\zeta)&\text{(quantile)},\\
\tau_{0}&\text{(absolute)},\end{cases}
``` |  | (16) |

where $`\mu_{i},\sigma_{i}`$ are empirical mean and standard deviation
of $`X_{i}`$, $`Q_{i}(\zeta)`$ is the $`\zeta`$-quantile, and
$`\tau_{0}`$ is a fixed constant (e.g., speed limits). The comparison
operator $`\prec`$ is selected to maximize safety separability between
the two sides of the threshold under safety outcome $`y\in\{0,1\}`$
(Section [4.1.2](#S4.SS1.SSS2 "4.1.2 Rule Compilation ‣ 4.1 Offline Preparation ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).

Graph Database Storage. Compiled rules are stored in a Neo4j graph
database \[[Webber, 2012](#bib.bib27)\] with a variable-indexed schema
(Figure [3](#A1.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ A.4.2 Rule Compilation Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Each rule node stores the complete tuple
$`(T,\mathcal{P}_{r},a_{r},c_{r})`$, where $`T`$ is the template type,
$`\mathcal{P}_{r}`$ contains calibrated predicates with concrete
thresholds $`\tau`$ and operators $`\prec`$, $`a_{r}`$ is the
recommended action, and $`c_{r}`$ is the confidence. Rule nodes connect
to variable nodes via TRIGGERS (input variables) and AFFECTS (output
variables) edges. This enables $`O(\log|\mathcal{R}|)`$ retrieval via
parameterized Cypher queries on variables active in the current state.
Database statistics are provided in
Appendix [C.7.1](#A3.SS7.SSS1 "C.7.1 Rule Database Statistics ‣ C.7 Compiled Rule Database ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Figure 3: Rule database schema.

#### A.4.3 Rule-Guided Planning Details

Scene-Aware Rule Retrieval. Given $`s_{t}\in\mathcal{S}`$, we identify
active variables $`\{X_{i}\}`$ and retrieve context-relevant rules via
parameterized Cypher queries \[[Francis et al., 2018](#bib.bib26)\]
generated by the LLM (read-only and schema-constrained;
Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
The retrieval map

|     |                                                               |     |      |
|-----|---------------------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       G:(\{X_{i}\},K)\longmapsto\mathcal{R}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
       ```                                                            |     | (17) |

returns up to $`K`$ rules ranked by confidence $`c_{r}`$. A rule $`r`$
is retrieved if its trigger variables
$`\{X_{j}\mid(X_{j},r)\in\texttt{TRIGGERS}\}`$ intersect with the active
set $`\{X_{i}\}`$ and the dependency direction satisfies the macro-group
constraints $`\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{H}}`$.

Action Scorer Architecture. The Transformer scorer \[[Liang et al.,
2022](#bib.bib29)\] takes state encoding $`\mathbf{x}_{t}`$ and rule
embedding $`\mathbf{u}_{r}`$ and emits relevance and action heads:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathbf{h}_{t,r}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\text{TransformerEnc}([\mathbf{x}_{t};\mathbf{u}_{r}]),`$ |  | (18) |
|  | $`\displaystyle S_{\text{rel}}(r,s_{t})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\sigma(\text{Dec}_{\text{rel}}(\mathbf{h}_{t,r}))\in[0,1],`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle S_{\text{act}}(r,s_{t},a)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\mathrm{softmax}(\text{Dec}_{\text{act}}(\mathbf{h}_{t,r}))_{a},`$ |  |  |

where $`S_{\text{rel}}`$ predicts whether $`r`$ should influence the
current decision, and $`S_{\text{act}}`$ predicts the action preference
given $`r`$. The combined rule-action score multiplies relevance, action
preference, and rule confidence:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
f(r,a\mid s_{t})=S_{\text{rel}}(r,s_{t})\cdot S_{\text{act}}(r,s_{t},a)\cdot c_{r}.
``` |  | (19) |

Scorer Training. The scorer is trained on expert trajectories
$`\{(s_{t},a^{*}_{t})\}`$ with a joint relevance + action objective:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}=\sum_{t,r}\big[\mathrm{BCE}(\mathrm{Exec}(r,s_{t}),S_{\text{rel}}(r,s_{t}))-\log S_{\text{act}}(r,s_{t},a^{*}_{t})\big],
``` |  | (20) |

where $`\mathrm{Exec}(r,s_{t})\in\{0,1\}`$ is the ground-truth rule
activation
(Eq. [2](#S3.E2 "Equation 2 ‣ Definition 3.6 (Executable Rule). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
and $`a^{*}_{t}`$ is the expert action.

Action Aggregation. The full noisy-OR action distribution corresponding
to
Eq. [5](#S4.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ 4.2.1 Rule-Guided Planning ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
is

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle P(a\mid s_{t})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\frac{1-\prod_{r\in\mathcal{R}_{t}(a)}\big(1-f(r,a\mid s_{t})\big)}{Z},`$ |  | (21) |
|  | $`\displaystyle a^{*}_{\text{rule}}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P(a\mid s_{t}),`$ |  |  |

where $`\mathcal{R}_{t}(a)\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{t}`$ denotes the rules
with recommended action $`a_{r}=a`$ and $`Z`$ normalizes over
$`\mathcal{A}`$. The formulation favors actions supported by multiple
high-confidence rules.

Trajectory Verification. For the top-$`M`$ actions ranked by
$`P(a\mid s_{t})`$, we predict $`H`$-step future states:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{s}_{t+h+1}=\Phi(\hat{s}_{t+h},a;\hat{\mathcal{G}},\hat{\mathcal{F}}),\quad h=0,\ldots,H-1,
``` |  | (22) |

where $`\Phi`$ applies each $`f_{i}\in\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$ to predict
$`X_{i}`$ from $`\mathrm{Pa}(X_{i})`$ under $`\hat{\mathcal{G}}`$. Each
rollout produces a candidate trajectory
$`\hat{\xi}(a)=(\hat{s}_{t+1},\ldots,\hat{s}_{t+H})`$, consistent with
Def. [3.3](#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Definition 3.3 (Dependency Graph and Dynamics). ‣ 3.2 World Dynamics Model ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").
The planner selects
$`a^{\star}_{t}=\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{M}}J(\hat{\xi}(a))`$, where
$`J:\mathcal{S}^{H}\to\mathbb{R}`$ penalizes collisions, rule
violations, and control effort
(Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Evaluation Metrics ‣ Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"));
the first control of the selected trajectory is executed in
receding-horizon \[[Zhang et al., 2021b](#bib.bib30)\].

#### A.4.4 Online Rule Acquisition Details

LLM-Driven Rule Proposal. On a miss, the harness queries $`\mathcal{M}`$
with the current state and active variables:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\tilde{r}=(\tilde{T},\tilde{\mathcal{P}},\tilde{a},\tilde{c})\;\sim\;\mathcal{M}(s_{t}),
``` |  | (23) |

where $`\tilde{r}`$ conforms to the rule template
(Def. [3.5](#S3.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 3.5 (Rule Template). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
and uses only variables in $`\mathcal{X}`$ and operators from the
predicate grammar
(Def. [3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Definition 3.4 (Predicate). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Prompt details are in
Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5 "A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Safe-Action Selection. When online rule acquisition rejects
$`\tilde{r}`$ and $`\mathcal{R}_{t}=\emptyset`$, the harness raises a
human-takeover alert and executes the action that maximizes minimum
lookahead time-to-collision under $`\hat{\mathcal{F}}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
a^{\mathrm{safe}}_{t}=\arg\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\;\min_{h\in[1,H]}\mathrm{TTC}\!\left(\hat{s}_{t+h}(a)\right).
``` |  | (24) |

Memory-Update Collision Branch.
Eq. [8](#S4.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ 4.2.2 Online Rule Acquisition ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")’s
collision branch averages confidences instead of creating a duplicate
node, so $`|\mathcal{R}|`$ counts distinct rule structures rather than
LLM-noise replicas. The hash $`\kappa`$ is computed over the structural
triple $`(\tilde{T},\tilde{\mathcal{P}},\tilde{a})`$ but excludes
$`\tilde{c}`$, so semantically identical proposals collide regardless of
confidence noise. Collisions are common during early deployment when
$`\mathcal{R}`$ is small, then taper off as coverage saturates.

Self-Evolution Properties.
Eq. [8](#S4.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ 4.2.2 Online Rule Acquisition ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
structurally enforces $`|\mathcal{R}_{t+1}|\geq|\mathcal{R}_{t}|`$
(memory monotonicity) and
$`\theta_{\mathcal{M}}\equiv\theta_{\mathcal{M}}^{(0)}`$ (no LLM
parameter update). Under stationarity of the scene distribution, the
per-state miss rate is non-increasing as $`\mathcal{R}`$ accumulates
coverage, since any state $`s`$ that triggered a miss and produced an
accepted $`\tilde{r}`$ is now matched by a rule keyed on the variables
active at $`s`$. Since $`\mathcal{M}`$ is invoked only on misses, its
per-scene invocation rate decays monotonically with
$`\Pr[\mathrm{Miss}]`$. We validate this behavior empirically in
Section [5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
and
Appendix [C.5](#A3.SS5 "C.5 Self-Evolution Across Scenarios ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### A.5 Prompts

We provide three prompt templates used in REINS’s offline compilation
pipeline.
Figure [4](#A1.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
shows the Variable Dependency Prompt, which instructs the LLM to infer
dependencies between driving variables based on domain knowledge; the
output serves as semantic priors for structure learning in the world
dynamics model.
Figure [5](#A1.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
presents the Rule Extraction Prompt, which converts dependency edges
into executable driving rules with parameterized guards and discrete
actions; these rules are stored in the Neo4j graph database.
Figure [6](#A1.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ A.5 Prompts ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
displays the Parameterized Cypher Query Prompt, which generates
parameterized queries for context-aware rule retrieval during planning.
All prompts enforce read-only constraints and structured output formats
to ensure safety and parseability.
````
