````math
## Appendix B Implementation Details

### B.1 Experiment Settings

Table [5](#A2.T5 "Table 5 ‣ B.1 Experiment Settings ‣ Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
summarizes the key hyperparameters and training configurations used in
our experiments. The settings are organized into six categories:
Simulation Environment specifies the highway-env simulator settings,
including four driving scenarios with varying complexity and a discrete
action space of five high-level maneuvers. Real-world Datasets describes
the highD (highway) and rounD (roundabout) drone datasets used for
real-world evaluation, including recording statistics and sampling
rates. World Dynamics Model details the structure learning parameters
(LLM prior weight, regularization) and neural network architecture
(Transformer layers, attention heads). Rule Compilation covers the
offline LLM-based rule generation process, including the backbone model,
sampling strategy, and threshold calibration. Training & Evaluation
specifies the data splits, random seeds, and hardware configuration.
Planning & Deployment reports inference-time parameters and latency
measurements demonstrating real-time capability. All hyperparameter
configurations follow standard practices unless otherwise stated.

|  |  |
|----|----|
| Category | Setting |
| Simulation Environment |  |
| Simulator | highway-env v1.8.2 |
| Scenarios | Highway, Merge, Roundabout, |
|  | Intersection |
| Episode length | 100 steps (20s at 5 Hz) |
| Observation | 15 nearest vehicles |
| Action space | {IDLE, FASTER, SLOWER, |
|  | LANE_LEFT, LANE_RIGHT} |
| Weather conditions | Clear, Rain, Fog, Snow |
| Real-world Datasets |  |
| highD source | German Autobahn (drone) |
| highD recordings | 01, 02, 03, 04 |
| highD vehicles | 4,237 (578 lane-change events) |
| rounD source | German roundabouts |
| rounD recordings | 00, 01, 02 |
| rounD vehicles | 2,156 (423 yield/merge events) |
| Sampling rate | 25 Hz |
| World Dynamics Model |  |
| Structure learning | Ridge regression + LLM prior |
| LLM prior weight $`\alpha`$ | 0.6 |
| Penalty exponent $`\rho`$ | 2.0 |
| Base regularization $`\lambda_{0}`$ | 0.1 |
| Lag length $`L`$ | 6 steps |
| Top-$`K`$ parents | 5 |
| Transformer layers | 2 |
| Attention heads | 4 |
| Optimizer | Adam |
| Learning rate | $`2\times 10^{-4}`$ |
| Batch size | 64 |
| Training episodes | 50k |
| Rule Compilation (Offline) |  |
| LLM backbone | GPT-4o (2024-08-06) |
| LLM temperature | 0.0 (deterministic) |
| Max output tokens | 4096 |
| Prompt tokens (avg.) | $`\sim`$1.2k per query |
| Total LLM calls | $`\sim`$50 (one-time offline) |
| Rule templates | 7 types |
| Neo4j version | 5.12 |
| Threshold calibration | Gaussian ($`\mu+\zeta\sigma`$), $`\zeta`$=1.5 |
| Confidence $`\tau`$ | 0.7 |
| Training & Evaluation |  |
| Training data | 50k episodes |
| Test episodes | 1000 per scenario |
| Random seeds | 5 (0, 42, 123, 456, 789) |
| Training hardware | 4$`\times`$ A100 (40GB) |
| Training time | $`\sim`$8 hours |
| Planning & Deployment |  |
| Retrieval count $`k`$ | 10 |
| Planning horizon $`H`$ | 5 steps |
| Rollout discount $`\gamma`$ | 0.99 |
| Safety weight $`\lambda_{\text{safe}}`$ | 1.0 |
| Comfort weight $`\lambda_{\text{comf}}`$ | 0.1 |
| Progress weight $`\lambda_{\text{prog}}`$ | 0.5 |
| Inference hardware | RTX 3090 or above |
| GPU memory | $`\sim`$4 GB |
| Neo4j query latency | $`<`$1 ms |
| Planning latency | 1.9–4.6 ms per decision |
| Real-time capability | 200–500 Hz |

Table 5: Experiment settings and hyperparameters for REINS.

### B.2 Evaluation Metrics

Rule Compliance (Compl.) measures the fraction of timesteps where all
active rules are satisfied:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{Compl.}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\prod_{k=1}^{K}\bigl(1-v_{k,t}\bigr),
``` |  | (25) |

where $`v_{k,t}\in\{0,1\}`$ indicates whether rule $`k`$ is violated at
time $`t`$.

Collision Rate (CR) measures the fraction of episodes in which at least
one collision occurs:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{CR}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{I}(\text{collision in episode }i),
``` |  | (26) |

where $`N`$ is the number of test episodes. A lower CR indicates safer
planning.

Risk-aware Efficiency (RE) balances task performance against safety:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | RE | $`\displaystyle=R_{\text{task}}(\tau)-\lambda_{\text{risk}}R_{\text{risk}}(\tau),`$ |  | (27) |
|  | $`\displaystyle R_{\text{risk}}(\tau)`$ | $`\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\max(0,\delta_{\text{ttc}}-\text{TTC}_{t}),`$ |  | (28) |

where $`R_{\text{task}}`$ is the task reward, $`R_{\text{risk}}`$
penalizes low TTC states, $`\lambda_{\text{risk}}=0.5`$ is the risk
penalty weight, and $`\delta_{\text{ttc}}=3.0`$s is the TTC threshold.

Planning Efficiency (PE) measures average computation time per decision:

|     |                                                     |     |      |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \text{PE}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}t_{\text{plan}},
       ```                                                  |     | (29) |

where $`t_{\text{plan}}`$ (ms) is the wall-clock time for action
selection.

Trajectory Objective $`J(\hat{\xi})`$. The trajectory-level objective
used in dynamics-based verification is defined as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle J(\hat{\xi})`$ | $`\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\gamma^{h-1}\Big[-\lambda_{\text{safe}}\cdot\mathbb{I}(\text{TTC}_{t+h}<\tau_{\text{ttc}})`$ |  | (30) |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle+\lambda_{\text{prog}}\cdot\Delta d_{t+h}-\lambda_{\text{comf}}\cdot|j_{t+h}|\Big],`$ |  |  |

where $`\text{TTC}_{t+h}`$ is the predicted time-to-collision,
$`\Delta d_{t+h}`$ is the progress toward the goal, $`j_{t+h}`$ is the
jerk, and $`\tau_{\text{ttc}}=2.0`$s is the safety threshold. The
weights $`\lambda_{\text{safe}}=1.0`$, $`\lambda_{\text{prog}}=0.5`$,
$`\lambda_{\text{comf}}=0.1`$ prioritize safety over progress and
comfort.

Real-world Evaluation Protocol. We use closed-loop replay: one recorded
vehicle is controlled by the planner while others follow recorded
trajectories. Collisions are detected when bounding boxes overlap or the
ego deviates beyond lane boundaries. In the simulated scenarios the
surrounding vehicles instead react to the ego (IDM with MOBIL); a
reactive-replay variant of highD is reported in
Appendix [D.6](#A4.SS6 "D.6 Reactive Replay and Observation Noise ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### B.3 Baseline Descriptions

We select baselines from three categories to comprehensively evaluate
REINS: (i) *classical planners* (MPC, MCTS) that represent traditional
optimization and search-based approaches without rule guidance; (ii)
*LLM-based planners* (DriveSpeak, PDDL, LLaDA, DriReg) that leverage
language models for driving decisions but differ in how they integrate
linguistic knowledge; and (iii) *hybrid methods* (SymAgent, Plan-R1)
that combine learning with symbolic reasoning. This selection covers the
spectrum from rule-free to rule-guided planning, enabling analysis of
how different rule representation and retrieval strategies affect safety
and efficiency.

- •
  MPC \[[Falcone et al., 2007](#bib.bib12)\]: Classical
  optimization-based planner solving finite-horizon control under
  kinematic and safety constraints. We use a 5-step horizon with
  quadratic cost on acceleration and jerk.
- •
  MCTS \[[Lenz et al., 2016](#bib.bib13)\]: Sampling-based planning via
  tree search and rollout evaluation. We use 1000 simulations per
  decision with UCB exploration.
- •
  DriveSpeak \[[Cui et al., 2024](#bib.bib14)\]: LLM-based method
  mapping natural-language scene descriptions directly to driving
  actions. Uses GPT-4o with scene prompts at each timestep.
- •
  PDDL \[[Guan et al., 2023](#bib.bib39), [Liu et al.,
  2023](#bib.bib40)\]: the LLM writes the domain’s symbolic
  preconditions and effects once offline; at runtime a classical STRIPS
  solver (Pyperplan \[[Alkhazraji et al., 2020](#bib.bib41)\]) is
  invoked in process at each decision step with no LLM call. Its runtime
  profile is in
  Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Runtime Profile of the PDDL Baseline ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").
- •
  LLaDA \[[Li et al., 2024a](#bib.bib1)\]: LLM interpretation of
  region-specific traffic rules from driver handbooks. Uses
  pre-extracted rule sets with LLM-based rule matching.
- •
  DriReg \[[Cai et al., 2024](#bib.bib2)\]: RAG-based traffic regulation
  retrieval with LLM reasoning for action legality. Retrieves top-5
  regulations per query using embedding similarity.
- •
  SymAgent \[[Liu et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)\]: Neural-symbolic agent
  integrating LLMs with structured knowledge graphs. Uses the same Neo4j
  backend as REINS for fair comparison.
- •
  Plan-R1 \[[Tang et al., 2025](#bib.bib4)\]: Two-stage learning-based
  planner with rule-shaped rewards and reinforcement learning. Trained
  on the same 50k episodes as REINS’s world model.

All baselines use identical observation spaces, action spaces, episodes,
and evaluation protocols. For LLM-based methods, we use GPT-4o
(2024-08-06) with temperature 0 for deterministic outputs.
Hyperparameters for each baseline follow their original papers or are
tuned on a held-out validation set. The conditions that are shared with
REINS and those that each rule-based baseline keeps by design, together
with a matched re-run and a common-reference compliance audit, are
documented in
Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

## Appendix C Additional Results

Figure 7: Training loss convergence for the World Dynamics Learning.
Each subplot shows one variable’s dynamics learning across four
scenarios (Highway, Intersection, Merge, Roundabout). All variables
converge within 150 epochs, with scenario-dependent convergence rates
reflecting inherent complexity differences.

### C.1 Training Convergence

We train the world dynamics model for 300 epochs using the Adam
optimizer with a learning rate of $`2\times 10^{-4}`$ and batch size 64.
Each variable-specific dynamics function $`f_{i}`$ is implemented as a
two-layer Transformer with four attention heads. Training is conducted
on 50k episodes collected from four scenarios (Highway, Intersection,
Merge, and Roundabout) using NVIDIA A100 GPUs. The model learns 20
variable-specific dynamics functions, including ego-state variables
(ego_speed, ego_acceleration, ego_vx, ego_vy, ego_x, ego_y, yaw_rate,
jerk, lane_id), traffic-related variables (lead_ttc, lead_thw,
lead_rel_speed, rear_rel_speed, lane_density_left, lane_density_right,
cutin_risk), and outcome variables (risk_score, comfort_score,
violation_flag, energy_consumption).
Figure [7](#A3.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
shows the training loss curves for all dynamics functions across the
four scenarios.

The results suggest that the learned world dynamics are
scenario-adaptive not only in difficulty but also in structural
composition: different scenarios activate and learn different subsets of
variable-specific dynamics functions. Interaction-heavy variables such
as cutin_risk, lane_density_left/right, and rear_rel_speed are prominent
in Merge and Intersection, where lane changes and merging behaviors are
frequent, but are weakly activated or effectively absent in Highway,
where traffic flow is comparatively stable. Conversely, lateral-motion
variables (e.g., ego_vy, yaw_rate) become substantially more relevant in
Roundabout due to persistent curvature, while contributing marginally in
straight-road scenarios. This indicates that the model does not impose a
fixed global mechanism set across environments, but instead learns
scenario-specific mechanism structures, supporting structural
adaptability to heterogeneous traffic contexts.

Beyond these structural differences, the loss curves exhibit consistent
convergence patterns. Most variables converge within 100–150 epochs.
Simple kinematic variables (e.g., ego_x, ego_y, lead_thw) converge
within 50 epochs, whereas interaction-intensive outcome variables (e.g.,
risk_score, violation_flag) require longer training. Highway achieves
the lowest final loss due to simpler lane-following dynamics. Roundabout
exhibits higher loss for lateral variables because of curved
trajectories, while Intersection shows higher variance in violation_flag
due to diverse right-of-way interactions. Safety-related variables
(e.g., lead_ttc, cutin_risk) converge rapidly in early training when
relevant interactions are present, indicating strong dependency signals.
In terms of final loss magnitude, continuous variables (e.g., ego_speed,
ego_acceleration) achieve losses below 0.5, whereas discrete or binary
variables (e.g., violation_flag, lane_id) stabilize at higher loss due
to their classification nature.

### C.2 Trajectory Accuracy

We evaluate trajectory accuracy using Average Displacement Error (ADE),
Final Displacement Error (FDE), and cumulative episode reward (Reward)
across four driving scenarios: Highway, Merge, Roundabout, and
Intersection. ADE and FDE measure the mean and final Euclidean distance
between predicted and executed trajectories, respectively, while Reward
reflects overall driving efficiency and safety. All methods are
evaluated under identical environment settings with the same observation
space, action space, and rollout horizon.

Table [6](#A3.T6 "Table 6 ‣ C.2 Trajectory Accuracy ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
reports the mean performance over all evaluation episodes. REINS
consistently achieves the best performance across all scenarios in terms
of both trajectory accuracy and cumulative reward. In Highway, REINS
reduces ADE and FDE to 15.9 and 20.5, respectively, outperforming the
strongest baseline (SymAgent) by 12.2% in ADE and 16.3% in FDE, while
also achieving the highest reward. Similar improvements are observed in
Merge and Roundabout, where REINS yields substantial gains in both
short-horizon and long-horizon prediction accuracy, indicating more
stable multi-step trajectory generation.

The performance gap becomes more pronounced in complex interaction
scenarios. In Intersection, REINS achieves an ADE of 5.6 and FDE of 9.7,
representing relative reductions of 30.0% and 13.4% compared to
SymAgent, and 41.7% (ADE) and 28.7% (FDE) compared to MPC. This suggests
that integrating rule-guided action selection with world-model-based
forward rollout is particularly effective when decision-making requires
anticipating multi-agent interactions and right-of-way constraints.

Across all scenarios, higher trajectory accuracy strongly correlates
with higher episode reward, confirming that more accurate future state
prediction leads to safer and more efficient planning. Methods relying
primarily on language-based reasoning (LLaDA) exhibit the largest
errors, especially in high-speed or curved-road settings, highlighting
the limitations of planning without explicit dynamics modeling. MPC
improves performance by incorporating learned motion predictors, while
SymAgent further benefits from symbolic constraints. REINS combines
symbolic rule guidance with learned world dynamics, enabling both
structured action filtering and predictive verification, which explains
its consistent superiority across heterogeneous traffic scenarios.

### C.3 Sensitivity to Retrieval Count $`k`$ and Planning Horizon $`H`$

We analyze sensitivity to two runtime hyperparameters: rule retrieval
count $`k`$ (the maximum number of rules returned per scene by the
Cypher query) and planning horizon $`H`$ (the rollout depth used by
trajectory verification). Both are evaluated on simulated (Highway,
Roundabout) and real-world (highD, rounD) scenarios.

Rule Retrieval Count $`k`$.
Table [9(a)](#A3.T9.st1 "Table 9(a) ‣ Table 9 ‣ C.3 Sensitivity to Retrieval Count 𝑘 and Planning Horizon 𝐻 ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
reports results for varying $`k`$. Small values ($`k\!\leq\!3`$) miss
critical rules, increasing CR by 3.5–4.7 points; large values
($`k\!\geq\!15`$) introduce redundancy, raising PE by 60–71% at
$`k{=}20`$ without safety gains. The optimal $`k{=}10`$ generalizes
across domains, with real-world and roundabout scenarios showing higher
sensitivity than structured highway settings.

Planning Horizon $`H`$.
Table [9(b)](#A3.T9.st2 "Table 9(b) ‣ Table 9 ‣ C.3 Sensitivity to Retrieval Count 𝑘 and Planning Horizon 𝐻 ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
reports results for varying $`H`$. Short horizons ($`H\!\leq\!3`$) cause
myopic decisions with 2.9–5.1 point CR increase; long horizons raise PE
by 35–44% at $`H{=}7`$ and roughly double it at $`H{=}10`$, with
marginal safety benefits. The optimal $`H{=}5`$ balances safety and
efficiency, though complex scenarios (Roundabout, rounD) require longer
horizons to anticipate multi-agent interactions.

### C.4 Sensitivity to Temporal Lag Length

The temporal lag length $`L`$ determines how many historical timesteps
the world dynamics model uses when predicting future states.
Table [7](#A3.T7 "Table 7 ‣ C.2 Trajectory Accuracy ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
reports results on both simulated and real-world scenarios.

When using small lag lengths ($`L\leq 2`$), the model fails to capture
cumulative temporal patterns such as sustained tailgating or gradual
lane drift, leading to substantially higher collision rates. On the
simulated Highway scenario, the collision rate increases from 5.67% at
$`L=6`$ to 9.34%, corresponding to a 65% relative increase. A similar
degradation is observed on the real-world highD dataset, where the
collision rate rises from 6.12% to 10.12%. These results indicate that
short-term observations are insufficient to infer latent interaction
states, such as risk accumulation and driver intent, which are critical
for safe planning. In contrast, large lag lengths ($`L\geq 10`$)
introduce excessive historical noise and dilute recent dependency
signals, reducing the effective signal-to-noise ratio for short-horizon
prediction. Consequently, collision rates increase by approximately 40%
on simulated scenarios compared to the optimal setting. This suggests
that overly long histories may impair the model’s ability to focus on
immediately relevant dynamics, particularly in rapidly evolving traffic
situations.

Overall, $`L=6`$ achieves the best and most consistent performance
across both simulated and real-world domains, corresponding to
approximately 1.2 seconds of historical context at a sampling rate of
5 Hz. This time window appears sufficient to capture short-term
interaction dynamics while avoiding the accumulation of outdated or
irrelevant information, thereby supporting stable multi-step rollout and
reliable safety verification in downstream planning.

### C.5 Self-Evolution Across Scenarios

This appendix reports self-evolution dynamics on the remaining simulated
scenarios (Merge, Roundabout, Intersection) and the real-world datasets
(highD, rounD), complementing the Highway results in
Section [5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").
For each scenario, we deploy REINS for 1000 episodes from the
offline-compiled memory $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$ and report
$`|\mathcal{R}|`$, LLM call rate, Compl., and CR at four deployment
checkpoints (ep. 250, 500, 750, 1000), with a Frozen $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$
row showing the same 1000-episode window run with acquisition disabled;
this row exposes $`|\mathcal{R}_{0}|`$ and isolates the contribution of
online rule writing from any aggregation effect of running more
episodes. The per-scenario $`|\mathcal{R}|`$ at ep. 1000 matches the
post-deployment totals in
Table [8](#A3.T8 "Table 8 ‣ C.2 Trajectory Accuracy ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Three patterns emerge across the five extended scenarios, mirroring the
Highway dynamics. First, $`|\mathcal{R}|`$ exhibits saturating growth:
approximately 44% of the rule additions (counted from
$`|\mathcal{R}_{0}|`$ in the Frozen row to $`|\mathcal{R}|`$ at ep.
1000) occur in the first 250 episodes, with diminishing returns
thereafter, so the LLM is invoked predominantly for previously unseen
scene clusters. Second, the LLM call rate decays monotonically in all
scenarios, from 68–79% in the first 250-episode window to 12–19% by ep.
1000 (a 4.2–5.5$`\times`$ reduction); scene complexity raises the
absolute size of $`\mathcal{R}`$ and the residual call rate (highest on
Intersection) but not the shape of the decay. Third, the Frozen
$`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$ control consistently underperforms the evolving
memory at ep. 1000 (mean Compl. gap $`\approx`$0.017, mean CR gap
$`\approx`$4.2 points), confirming that the gains attributable to online
acquisition are not artifacts of statistical aggregation over a longer
evaluation window. Intersection benefits most (Compl. $`+0.0218`$, CR
$`-4.87`$ points from Frozen to ep. 1000), reflecting larger room for
online acquisition where the offline $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$ is most
incomplete relative to right-of-way reasoning; the other four scenarios
cluster in a tight range (Compl. $`+0.0154`$ to $`+0.0164`$, CR
$`-3.79`$ to $`-4.40`$ points). highD mirrors Highway and rounD mirrors
Roundabout in both $`|\mathcal{R}|`$ growth shape and Compl./CR
convergence, indicating that the harness operates on factored variable
signatures rather than scenario-specific surface features. Cross-domain
transfer of the compiled memory itself is studied separately in
Appendix [D.7](#A4.SS7 "D.7 Cross-Domain Memory Transfer ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### C.6 Case Study

Figure [9](#A3.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ C.6 Case Study ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
compares trajectories of REINS, Plan-R1, LLaDA, and MPC on two
scenarios, with activated rules and actions shown at each timestep. Rule
definitions are provided in
Appendix [C.7.2](#A3.SS7.SSS2 "C.7.2 Representative Rules ‣ C.7 Compiled Rule Database ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

Simulated Highway. REINS activates R03 and R07 at $`t{=}0`$ to monitor
the lead vehicle and adjacent lanes, triggers R12 at $`t{=}0.5`$s when a
safe gap appears, executes a lane change at $`t{=}1.0`$s, and
accelerates via R15 at $`t{=}1.5`$s, producing a smooth action sequence
(IDLE$`\!\to\!`$IDLE$`\!\to\!`$RIGHT$`\!\to\!`$FASTER). Plan-R1 applies
all 15 rules simultaneously, causing conflicting constraints and
repeated deceleration (SLOWER$`\!\times\!3`$) before a delayed maneuver.

Real-World highD. In the cut-in scenario, REINS detects lateral
intrusion at $`t{=}0`$ via R05, performs preemptive deceleration at
$`t{=}0.5`$s using R07, and maintains safe distance until $`t{=}1.5`$s
(IDLE$`\!\to\!`$SLOWER$`\!\to\!`$SLOWER$`\!\to\!`$IDLE). Plan-R1
responds later with emergency braking at $`t{=}1.0`$s due to uniform
rule execution.

![Refer to caption](2611.10748v1/case_study-v2.png)

Figure 9: Trajectory comparison across four methods on (a) simulated
highway and (b) real-world highD scenarios.

### C.7 Compiled Rule Database

| ID | Guard Condition $`\mathcal{P}(r)`$ | Action $`a_{r}`$ | Template |
|----|----|----|----|
| R01 | $`\text{TTC}<2.0\text{s}`$ | emergency brake | THRESHOLD |
| R02 | $`v_{ego}>v_{limit}+5\text{km/h}`$ | DEC to limit | THRESHOLD |
| R03 | $`\text{THW}<1.8\text{s}\lor\text{DHW}<25\text{m}`$ | DEC / maintain gap | IF-THEN |
| R04 | $`\text{TTC}<3.5\text{s}\land\text{DHW}<40\text{m}`$ | DEC (moderate) | AND-THEN |
| R05 | $`v^{lat}_{adj}>0.3\text{m/s}\land\Delta y<2\text{m}`$ | DEC (preemptive) | AND-THEN |
| R06 | $`\text{visibility}<100\text{m}`$ | reduce speed 20% | THRESHOLD |
| R07 | $`\text{TTC}_{adj}<4\text{s}\lor\text{cut-in}`$ | increase headway | IF-THEN |
| R08 | $`\text{lane\_end}<200\text{m}`$ | prepare merge | TEMPORAL-CHAIN |
| R09 | $`\text{at\_yield}\land\text{traffic\_present}`$ | yield / stop | PRIORITY |
| R10 | $`\text{pedestrian}<30\text{m}`$ | DEC / prepare stop | MULTI-ACTION |
| R11 | $`v_{lead}<v_{ego}-10\text{km/h}\land\text{gap}_{adj}>35\text{m}`$ | consider lane change | AND-THEN |
| R12 | $`\text{gap}_{target}>30\text{m}\land\text{TTC}_{target}>5\text{s}`$ | execute lane change | AND-THEN |
| R13 | $`\text{in\_lane\_change}\land\text{TTC}_{new}<3\text{s}`$ | abort lane change | AND-THEN |
| R14 | $`\text{merge\_complete}\land\text{DHW}<20\text{m}`$ | DEC post-merge | AND-THEN |
| R15 | $`\text{post\_merge}\land v_{ego}<v_{flow}-5\text{km/h}`$ | ACC to flow | AND-THEN |

Table 11: Representative rules from the compiled rule database.

#### C.7.1 Rule Database Statistics

Table [8](#A3.T8 "Table 8 ‣ C.2 Trajectory Accuracy ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
shows the distribution of variables by macro-group and rules by template
type in REINS’s Neo4j graph database. The post-deployment database
contains 1,023 rules covering six driving scenarios (four simulated:
Highway, Merge, Roundabout, Intersection; two real-world: highD, rounD).
IF-THEN and AND-THEN templates dominate due to the prevalence of
conditional and multi-condition reasoning in driving. Intersection
scenarios contain the most rules due to complex right-of-way
interactions, while Highway scenarios require fewer given simpler
lane-following dynamics. This scale is comparable to industrial
rule-based systems while remaining tractable for $`O(\log n)`$ graph
database retrieval ($`<`$1 ms per query).

Figure [8](#A3.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ C.2 Trajectory Accuracy ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
visualizes the graph structure of 200 randomly sampled rules from the
database. Gray nodes are variables, blue nodes are rules, and pink nodes
are the five discrete actions. Edge colors match source node colors:
gray edges represent Variable$`\to`$Rule, while blue edges represent
Rule$`\to`$Action.

Several structural patterns emerge from this visualization: (1) Variable
sharing: A small set of variables (e.g., TTC, THW, speed) triggers a
large number of rules, indicating that safety-critical variables serve
as common preconditions across diverse driving scenarios. (2) Action
concentration: The majority of rules converge to SLOWER and IDLE, which
exhibit the highest in-degree among all five actions. This reflects the
safety-first design principle where conservative actions are recommended
under uncertain or risky conditions. In contrast, LANE_LEFT, LANE_RIGHT,
and FASTER receive fewer rule connections, as these actions require more
stringent preconditions. (3) Rule diversity: Despite action
concentration, rules maintain diverse trigger conditions from different
variables, enabling fine-grained context-aware activation rather than
uniform rule application. These patterns support REINS’s retrieval
strategy: variable-indexed queries efficiently activate relevant rule
subsets, while action aggregation via noisy-OR naturally prioritizes
safety-critical recommendations.

#### C.7.2 Representative Rules

Table [11](#A3.T11 "Table 11 ‣ C.7 Compiled Rule Database ‣ Appendix C Additional Results ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
lists 15 representative rules from REINS’s compiled rule database. The
full post-deployment Neo4j database contains 1,023 rules across six
driving scenarios, from which context-relevant subsets are retrieved at
runtime via parameterized Cypher queries. These rules are automatically
compiled from LLM-generated linguistic descriptions and calibrated using
the threshold calibration mechanism described in
Section [4.1.2](#S4.SS1.SSS2 "4.1.2 Rule Compilation ‣ 4.1 Offline Preparation ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").
For fair comparison, Plan-R1 applies the same 15 representative rules
uniformly at each step, while REINS selectively activates 2–3
context-relevant rules via the retrieval mechanism.

The 15 rules cover six of the seven template types defined in
Definition [3.5](#S3.Thmtheorem5 "Definition 3.5 (Rule Template). ‣ 3.3 Rule Representation ‣ 3 Preliminaries ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"):
(1) THRESHOLD rules (R01, R02, R06) enforce numeric boundaries on
physical variables such as TTC and speed; (2) IF-THEN rules (R03, R07)
implement basic conditional logic with disjunctive guards; (3) AND-THEN
rules (R04, R05, R11–R15) require multiple conditions to be satisfied
simultaneously; (4) PRIORITY rules (R09) establish decision hierarchies
for right-of-way scenarios; (5) MULTI-ACTION rules (R10) trigger
compound actions under critical conditions; (6) TEMPORAL-CHAIN rules
(R08) encode time-sensitive constraints for upcoming events. The
complete definitions of all seven rule template types are provided in
Appendix [A.2](#A1.SS2 "A.2 Rule Templates ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"),
and the conflict resolution mechanism is described in
Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3 "A.3 Rule Conflict Resolution ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

## Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability

This appendix collects the studies that scope the harness claim and
stress the main results: the domain-specific versus domain-agnostic
components of the harness
([D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Scope of the Harness Claim ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
the baseline fairness protocol with a matched re-run and a
common-reference compliance audit
([D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
the runtime profile of the PDDL baseline
([D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Runtime Profile of the PDDL Baseline ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
robustness to the LLM backbone and to prompt variation
([D.4](#A4.SS4 "D.4 Robustness to the LLM Backbone and to Prompt Variation ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
what rollout verification filters
([D.5](#A4.SS5 "D.5 What Rollout Verification Filters ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
reactive replay and observation noise
([D.6](#A4.SS6 "D.6 Reactive Replay and Observation Noise ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
cross-domain memory transfer
([D.7](#A4.SS7 "D.7 Cross-Domain Memory Transfer ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
the latency of the online-miss cycle and its scheduling
([D.8](#A4.SS8 "D.8 Latency of the Online-Miss Cycle and Its Scheduling ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
and retrieval at deployment scale
([D.9](#A4.SS9 "D.9 Retrieval and Aggregation at Deployment Scale ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Unless stated otherwise, all studies use the Highway scenario, five
seeds, and the protocol of
Section [5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 Experiment Setting ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### D.1 Scope of the Harness Claim

The three couplings are presented as a harness pattern, while the
implementation is specialized to driving.
Table [12](#A4.T12 "Table 12 ‣ D.1 Scope of the Harness Claim ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
classifies each component of REINS and sketches the instantiation for a
different trajectory-planning domain, robot-arm manipulation, whose
action space is continuous and high-dimensional and whose safety notions
(contact force, grasp stability) differ from driving. Two components are
domain schemas that any instantiation must author; the remaining five
transfer unchanged. The safety notion enters only through the variable
schema and the terms of the objective $`J`$: calibrated predicates over
contact-force variables are structurally identical to TTC guards, and
the calibration procedure and the verification protocol do not change.
The harness contribution lives in the five unchanged components; the two
redesigned ones constitute the domain description that any planner for
that domain must author anyway.

|  Component |  Driving (this paper) |  Manipulation (sketch) |  Transfer |
|----|----|----|----|
|  Variable space and groups |  44 variables in Env, Traffic, Ego, Action, Outcome |  joint angles and torques, end-effector pose, contact forces, object poses |  redesign |
|  Action space |  5 driving meta-actions |  skill primitives (reach, grasp, lift, place, retreat) |  redesign |
|  Predicate grammar and thresholds |  TTC and THW guards |  force-margin, slip, and grasp-stability guards |  grammar unchanged, thresholds recalibrated |
|  Dynamics learning |  LLM prior, group-weighted ridge, per-variable Transformer |  same, retrained on domain data |  unchanged |
|  Verification |  collision and violation terms in $`J`$ |  contact-force and stability terms in $`J`$ |  protocol unchanged |
|  Indexed memory and retrieval |  variable-keyed graph schema |  same |  unchanged |
|  Acquisition loop |  miss detection, proposal, verification, write-back |  same |  unchanged |

Table 12: Domain-specific and domain-agnostic components of the harness.

### D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol

Shared and unshared conditions. PDDL, DriReg, and LLaDA were conceived
for symbolic planning domains, regulatory text, and driver handbooks
respectively, none with a factored driving state, so each was
re-implemented faithfully to its original design for closed-loop
highway-env.
Table [13](#A4.T13 "Table 13 ‣ D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
lists what is shared with REINS and what each method keeps by design.
The shared conditions fix the observation space, action space, episodes,
LLM backbone, and decoding; the unshared ones leave each method its own
knowledge representation and thresholds, which leaves the
rule-generation procedure as a confound. Three instruments below isolate
the harness regardless.

|  Condition | PDDL | DriReg | LLaDA |
|----|----|----|----|
|  Observation space, action space, episodes | shared | shared | shared |
|  GPT-4o backbone (2024-08-06), temperature 0 for rule generation | shared | shared | shared |
|  44-variable vocabulary (Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1 "A.1 Variable Definitions ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")) in rule-generation prompts | own vocabulary | own vocabulary | n/a (linguistic rules) |
|  Threshold calibration | uncalibrated | uncalibrated | n/a (no thresholds) |

Table 13: Fairness conditions for the rule-based baselines as published.
“Own vocabulary” and “uncalibrated” follow each method’s original
design.

Matched re-run. We regenerated the PDDL and DriReg rules over the same
44-variable vocabulary, passed their thresholds through REINS’s
calibration pipeline, and re-evaluated
(Table [14](#A4.T14 "Table 14 ‣ D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Input normalization improves both baselines by 3–5 CR points and
explains a minority of the gap; the majority survives it.

| CR (%)       | PDDL  | PDDL matched | DriReg | DriReg matched | REINS |
|--------------|-------|--------------|--------|----------------|-------|
| Highway      | 25.93 | 21.37        | 21.76  | 18.92          | 5.67  |
| Intersection | 32.12 | 27.64        | 29.89  | 25.81          | 9.08  |

Table 14: Matched re-run: PDDL and DriReg rules regenerated over REINS’s
variable vocabulary with calibrated thresholds.

Bracketing with the ablations. Two ablations from
Table [2](#S5.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 5.2 Performance Evaluation ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
bracket the remaining gap with the paper’s own numbers. Hand-crafted
rules inside the full harness still reach CR 11.23% on Highway, better
than every matched no-harness variant in
Table [14](#A4.T14 "Table 14 ‣ D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
and close to the best baseline Plan-R1 at 12.83%. Conversely, applying
REINS’s rules uniformly, the way the baselines use rules, raises CR by
22–43% and PE by 34–58%. Worse inputs with the harness keep most of the
gain; the same inputs without the harness lose it.

Common-reference compliance. Compliance in
Table [1](#S5.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 5.1 Experiment Setting ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
is measured against each method’s own rule set.
Table [15](#A4.T15 "Table 15 ‣ D.2 Baseline Fairness Protocol ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
re-scores every method against REINS’s compiled rule set as a common
reference. Compliance is a property of the executed trajectory rather
than of the planner: at each step, the reference rules whose guards hold
in the logged state become active, and a violation is counted when the
logged behavior fails an active rule
(Appendix [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 Evaluation Metrics ‣ Appendix B Implementation Details ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Each method therefore plans exactly as before and its trajectories are
audited afterward, which is also how the four methods without native
rule sets receive values. Because the reference set equals REINS’s
planning set, REINS’s own value is unchanged by construction; the
informative content is that the baseline ordering and the gaps persist.

| Method     | Own rules |              | Common reference (REINS set) |              |
|------------|-----------|--------------|------------------------------|--------------|
|            | Highway   | Intersection | Highway                      | Intersection |
| MPC        | n/a       | n/a          | 0.8712                       | 0.8423       |
| MCTS       | n/a       | n/a          | 0.8831                       | 0.8517       |
| DriveSpeak | n/a       | n/a          | 0.8246                       | 0.7912       |
| LLaDA      | n/a       | n/a          | 0.8412                       | 0.8104       |
| PDDL       | 0.9123    | 0.8789       | 0.8867                       | 0.8621       |
| DriReg     | 0.9452    | 0.9153       | 0.9083                       | 0.8874       |
| SymAgent   | 0.9561    | 0.9274       | 0.9312                       | 0.9067       |

| REINS      | 0.9956    | 0.9838       | 0.9956                       | 0.9838       |

REINS’s compiled rule set as a common reference.

### D.3 Runtime Profile of the PDDL Baseline

The PDDL baseline follows the pipeline in which the LLM writes the
domain’s preconditions and effects offline and a classical solver
executes at runtime \[[Guan et al., 2023](#bib.bib39), [Liu et al.,
2023](#bib.bib40)\]. The solver is Pyperplan \[[Alkhazraji et al.,
2020](#bib.bib41)\], a Python STRIPS planner invoked in process at each
decision step; there is no per-step LLM call. Its 4.95 ms per step on
Highway
(Table [1](#S5.T1 "Table 1 ‣ 5.1 Experiment Setting ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
decomposes into grounding 3.71 ms, search 0.94 ms, and plan extraction
0.30 ms. The solver re-instantiates every precondition against the
current state at every step with no indexing, so grounding takes roughly
three quarters of the budget. REINS avoids exactly this cost:
variable-indexed retrieval touches 2–3 rules in under 1 ms, followed by
a fixed-size rollout at $`H{=}5`$. This is the architectural payoff of
consolidation with indexing.

### D.4 Robustness to the LLM Backbone and to Prompt Variation

The LLM produces the dependency priors, the rule structures, and the
Cypher queries, so we regenerate the whole offline pipeline with two
further backbones and with three paraphrases of the dependency and
rule-extraction prompts
(Table [16](#A4.T16 "Table 16 ‣ D.4 Robustness to the LLM Backbone and to Prompt Variation ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Rule structure varies mildly across backbones and paraphrases, while the
calibrated thresholds and the final behavior barely move. The LLM picks
variables, templates, and actions; calibration re-anchors every numeric
threshold to data, so backbone differences wash out at the threshold
level. Malformed or unsafe proposals never reach memory, because rollout
verification rejects them regardless of which backbone or prompt
produced them.

[TABLE]

Table 16: Robustness of the compiled rules and of closed-loop behavior
to the LLM backbone and to prompt paraphrases.

### D.5 What Rollout Verification Filters

On the 1000-episode Highway deployment of
Section [5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"),
applying the windowed call rates of
Table [3](#S5.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
to the 100,000 deployment steps yields roughly 39,000 logged proposals
(an arithmetic consequence of the call rates, not an independent count).
Of these, 2.1% failed schema validation, 18.0% were rejected by rollout
verification, and 79.9% were accepted. Acceptances overwhelmingly
hash-collide with rules already in memory
(Eq. [8](#S4.E8 "Equation 8 ‣ 4.2.2 Online Rule Acquisition ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
and only update confidences; they deduplicate to the 57 unique rules
that grow the memory from 88 to 145. A manual audit of the 57 unique
rules judged 54 consistent with domain guidelines; the three flagged
rules were over-specific and never activated again. Episodes in which at
least one acquired rule fired show no elevated collision rate against
episodes in which none fired (5.4% against 5.8%).

Three properties of the design bound what a plausible but incorrect
proposal can do. Verification errs toward rejection: a rule is accepted
only if every calibrated predicate holds along the predicted horizon
(Eq. [7](#S4.E7 "Equation 7 ‣ 4.2.2 Online Rule Acquisition ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
so dynamics error costs availability rather than safety in the common
case. Accepted rules never bypass the runtime loop: every executed
action is re-verified by rollout at decision time
(Section [4.2.1](#S4.SS2.SSS1 "4.2.1 Rule-Guided Planning ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")),
so a wrongly accepted rule must also survive the per-step check before
it can affect control. Finally, the residual dependence on dynamics
fidelity is stated in
Section [6](#S6 "6 Limitations ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### D.6 Reactive Replay and Observation Noise

Reactive replay. In the four simulated scenarios the surrounding
vehicles already react to the ego through IDM car-following with MOBIL
lane changes. The real-world rows keep highD and rounD as recorded so
that they reflect human behavior rather than a simulator’s. To measure
the effect of this choice, a reactive-replay variant on highD switches
recorded vehicles within 50 m of the ego to IDM once interaction occurs,
following the reactive closed-loop protocol of nuPlan \[[Caesar et al.,
2021](#bib.bib42)\]. REINS CR moves from 6.12% to 6.94% while Plan-R1
moves from 12.78% to 15.36%, so the gap to the strongest baseline
widens.

Observation noise. We corrupt the observed variables with zero-mean
Gaussian noise scaled to each variable’s standard deviation and,
separately, drop 5% of frames
(Table [17](#A4.T17 "Table 17 ‣ D.6 Reactive Replay and Observation Noise ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Calibrated thresholds carry a data-derived margin and every action is
vetted by a multi-step rollout rather than by a single reading, so REINS
degrades more slowly than the strongest baseline at every level, with
Compl. staying at or above 0.987. Full-stack perception errors
(occlusion, tracking identity switches, map errors) remain out of scope.

| CR (%) on Highway | no noise | 2.5% noise | 5% noise | 10% noise | 5% frame drops |
|-------------------|----------|------------|----------|-----------|----------------|
| REINS             | 5.67     | 5.92       | 6.51     | 8.14      | 6.88           |
| Plan-R1           | 12.83    | 13.71      | 15.42    | 19.87     | 16.50          |

Table 17: Collision rate under $`\sigma`$-scaled Gaussian observation
noise and under frame drops.

A domain-shifted memory starts degraded: at ep. 250 the call rate runs
86.2% on Highway against the native 70.0% of
Table [3](#S5.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"),
and CR runs several points above the native value at the same checkpoint
(11.84% against 7.05%). Acquisition recovers most of the gap within 1000
episodes: final CR lands within 0.5–1.1 points of native and Compl.
within 0.003–0.006. On Highway, freezing the transferred memory prevents
recovery: at ep. 1000 the frozen transfer stays at CR 11.20% against
6.21% with acquisition enabled, so the recovery comes from
verification-gated acquisition rather than from the transferred rules
themselves, mirroring the native pattern of
Table [3](#S5.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 5.4 Self-Evolution Analysis ‣ 5 Experiment ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"),
where frozen $`\mathcal{R}_{0}`$ ends at CR 9.63% against the evolved
5.67%. Sim-to-real shows slightly smaller residual gaps and lower final
call rates than real-to-sim; one plausible factor is its larger source
memory (432 against 187 rules before deduplication).

The initial call rate of 86.2% also shows that most of the source-domain
memory is not functionally reusable zero-shot in a new but still driving
context. This is consistent with the safety-oriented design: thresholds
are calibrated to the source-domain distribution, so under shift the
harness re-invokes and re-verifies rather than reusing rules whose
calibration may no longer hold. We therefore do not frame these results
as evidence of strong zero-shot generalization; the limitation is stated
in
Section [6](#S6 "6 Limitations ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning").

### D.8 Latency of the Online-Miss Cycle and Its Scheduling

What PE counts. For every method, PE measures the synchronous
action-selection computation per step. In the evaluated closed-loop
setting, a miss completes proposal, verification, and write-back within
the step while simulation time is paused, so the reported PE excludes
the acquisition pipeline, namely the LLM call together with the
subsequent parsing, calibration, verification, and write-back.
Table [19](#A4.T19 "Table 19 ‣ D.8 Latency of the Online-Miss Cycle and Its Scheduling ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
profiles each stage.

| Stage | Mean | p95 | Deployment scheduling |
|----|----|----|----|
| Fallback action for the current step | 2.6 ms | 3.9 ms | control path, within budget |
| Prompt assembly and state serialization | 0.9 ms | 1.4 ms | background |
| LLM rule proposal (GPT-4o API) | 1.58 s | 2.94 s | background |
| Parsing and schema validation | 2.1 ms | 3.0 ms | background |
| Threshold calibration on the sliding window | 1.3 ms | 1.9 ms | background |
| Rollout verification at $`H{=}5`$ | 1.6 ms | 2.2 ms | background |
| Neo4j insert and index update | 4.7 ms | 6.8 ms | background |
| Full cycle, miss to usable rule | 1.59 s | 2.96 s | background |

Table 19: Latency of the online-miss cycle on Highway. The fallback row
sits on the control path and is not part of the full-cycle latency; the
acquisition stages below it sum to the full-cycle row.

Deployment scheduling. The cycle takes 1.59 s mean, dominated by the LLM
call; the acquisition stages other than the LLM call total about 11 ms.
In deployment the cycle moves off the critical path by construction,
because the planner has a defined behavior without the acquired rule:
the best action over the retrieved rules or, with empty retrieval, the
safe action that maximizes minimum lookahead TTC
(Eq. [24](#A1.E24 "Equation 24 ‣ A.4.4 Online Rule Acquisition Details ‣ A.4 Method Details ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
together with a human-takeover alert. The current step executes that
fallback while the cycle completes in the background, and the acquired
rule serves later encounters. At 5 Hz the acquisition window spans about
eight control cycles, during which the planner behaves as a verified
conservative planner.
Figure [10](#A4.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ D.8 Latency of the Online-Miss Cycle and Its Scheduling ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
contrasts the evaluated schedule with the deployment schedule.

Figure 10: Dataflow of the online-miss cycle. Top: in the evaluated
closed-loop setting the cycle completes within the step while simulation
time is paused, and PE counts only the synchronous control-path
computation. Bottom: in deployment the current step executes the
verified fallback while the cycle runs in the background (about eight
control cycles at 5 Hz), and the acquired rule serves later encounters.

### D.9 Retrieval and Aggregation at Deployment Scale

As memory grows, retrieval and multi-rule aggregation must stay within
the control window. The stress test runs on Highway and varies only the
database size, inflating it with synthetic rules generated as variable
and threshold perturbations of compiled rules that preserve the schema
and the trigger-degree distribution
(Table [20](#A4.T20 "Table 20 ‣ D.9 Retrieval and Aggregation at Deployment Scale ‣ Appendix D Fairness Protocol, Robustness, Transfer, and Scalability ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")).
Retrieval stays sub-millisecond and grows slowly with memory size, from
0.41 ms at the paper’s 1,023 rules to 0.79 ms at one hundred thousand,
consistent with indexed lookup rather than linear scanning. Conflict
aggregation does not grow with the database: retrieval returns at most
$`k{=}10`$ rules regardless of memory size, so noisy-OR aggregation
(Eq. [5](#S4.E5 "Equation 5 ‣ 4.2.1 Rule-Guided Planning ‣ 4.2 Online Runtime ‣ 4 Methodology ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning"))
and the arbitration of
Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3 "A.3 Rule Conflict Resolution ‣ Appendix A More Details about REINS ‣ REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning")
cost $`O(k)`$. On the online planning path, the database-size-dependent
cost therefore concentrates in retrieval; index maintenance and write
costs sit on the acquisition path, off the control loop.

| Memory size           | 1,023 (paper) | 10,000 | 50,000 | 100,000 |
|-----------------------|---------------|--------|--------|---------|
| Cypher retrieval (ms) | 0.41          | 0.55   | 0.68   | 0.79    |
| End-to-end PE (ms)    | 2.50          | 2.64   | 2.77   | 2.88    |

Table 20: Retrieval latency and end-to-end PE on Highway as the rule
memory is inflated with schema-preserving synthetic rules.
````
